### Games, instruments and performance

<a id="games-instruments-and-performance"></a>

In-character entertainment: playable instruments, arcade and tabletop games, physical card decks and station radio broadcasting.

**estacao-pirata**

*activity · drop-in · high confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Adds a real deck of playing cards as a physical object: a deck case that pops open into a full 52-card deck plus a joker, each card an individual item you can pick up, drop, rotate and flip face-up or face-down. Right-clicking a deck lets you shuffle it, cut it in two, or turn the whole thing face up or face down, all with card-riffle and card-slap sounds. Stack loose cards together into a "hand" you hold, open it to see the cards fanned out in a menu and pick the exact one you want to play, or fold a hand back down into a deck. Nothing is scored or enforced by the game, so poker, blackjack or a bar card trick is played the way people play it at a table, by agreement.

- **Take** — It is a complete, self-contained toy for downtime roleplay: bartenders, prisoners and idle crew get a tactile card table that runs entirely on player agreement, with no balance surface to tune.
- **Skip** — It carries roughly 55 card entity prototypes plus an RSI and a folder of card audio for a purely optional pastime, and enforces no rules at all, so a table of cards only entertains players already inclined to invent a game; getting decks into players' hands also means editing an upstream vendor, loadout or map spawn.
- **Touches** — service, all-players, command, security
- **Verified** — partly supported. I read all four cited files plus the stack/card systems and the full repo tree: every card file (C#, YAML, audio, RSI, locale) sits under _EstacaoPirata/EstacaoPirata/estacao-pirata directories and imports only upstream Content.Shared/Robust APIs, so drop-in holds; the 52+joker deck, per-card flip/rotate, shuffle/organize verbs, hand menu, and convert-to-deck are all real, but the "cut it in two" behavior is contradicted by the code (full-count transfer, not a halving) and the unit silently depends on the fork's separate OpenTriggeredStorageFill system.
  - unsupported: "cut it in two" — the split verb's locale text says "Split in half", but CardDeckSystem.TrySplit passes stack.Cards.Count (the FULL count) to TransferNLastCardFromStacks, so the code transfers every card into a new deck and the emptied original is deleted; nothing is halved in the shipped behavior.
  - unsupported: depends_on: [] — the deck case's pop-open fill relies on OpenTriggeredStorageFillComponent, implemented in a separate fork system (Content.Server/_EstacaoPirata/OpenTriggeredStorageFill/, 2 files) that is not in the cited evidence or the dependency list; without copying it the boxed-deck experience described in the first sentence does not work.

**sunrise**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Three separate entertainment tweaks bundled together. The headline one is a tape player: you slot a music cassette into a player, then a small window lets you hit play, pause and stop, scrub to a point in the track, and set the volume, with each tape carrying its own named song and length. The music is positional and fades out past roughly twenty tiles, so a bar or dorm can have background music that people actually have to walk toward. Second, non-standard dice get a right-click option that opens a window where you type the lowest and highest number the die can roll, so a gambler or a game master can reshape a die on the spot instead of hunting for the right one. Third, instrument playing is tightened up: a handheld instrument only responds if it is genuinely in your hands, you have to be able to reach and see it, joining someone else's band needs line of sight, and the server throws out malformed or spammed MIDI input so nobody can lag the room with a junk file.

- **Take** — The tape player gives bartenders, chaplains and dorm-dwellers ambient music without anyone needing to know MIDI, and the instrument hardening quietly kills the classic "someone uploads a garbage MIDI and tanks the server" griefing vector.
- **Skip** — It is three unrelated mini-features stapled into one unit, and the two most useful parts are not self-contained: the instrument mitigation is written as partial-class extensions of upstream's InstrumentComponent and InstrumentSystem (upstream must be made partial and must call into the fork's validation on every MIDI path), and the dice verb keys off an IsNotStandardDice field added to upstream's DiceComponent. Every upstream sync risks re-breaking those seams.
- **Touches** — service, all-players
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Character records

<a id="character-records"></a>

Player-authored medical, security and employment records written in the character editor and read or edited in-round from dedicated consoles.

**serbia-strong**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Instead of one flag per crewmember, security keeps a running case file on each person: every entry is a written note stamped with a time and one of nine statuses (wanted, search, monitor, execute, demote, paroled, incarcerated, discharged, suspected), each with its own coloured icon floating over that person's head for anyone in a security HUD. Marking someone wanted, suspected, to be executed or to be demoted automatically broadcasts a report on the security channel, so the department hears it without anyone typing. An officer wearing a security HUD can right-click a crewmember in the hallway and open their whole file on the spot, read the history and add a new status, rather than walking back to the console. The same bundle adds coloured microfibers to forensics, so swabbing an object can turn up "black leather microfibers" or "holographic chameleon microfibers" that a scanner later matches to a suspect's clothing.

- **Take** — It turns security bookkeeping into actual play: a note-and-history file readable from the hallway, nine meaningful statuses with distinct HUD icons, and auto-broadcast alerts, which is a large upgrade over upstream's four-state flag.
- **Skip** — Every SS220 file carries a hosting-restricted EULA/CLA header rather than an open licence, which collides head-on with the fork's REUSE/AGPL policy, and the feature also replaces upstream's own criminal records console, so it is a rip-and-replace of a system upstream keeps changing.
- **Touches** — security, command, all-players
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**sunrise**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Instead of upstream's simple "set status, add a crime line" security console, Sunrise gives security a full case-file workflow: an officer pulls up a person's record (name, job, age, species, fingerprints, DNA, current status), opens a numbered criminal case, ticks which corporate law articles were broken plus aggravating or mitigating circumstances, writes notes, and the console totals a recommended sentence in minutes with an itemised breakdown the warden can read line by line. Closed cases feed a separate prisoner management console showing who is queued for jail, who is currently serving time in which of ten cells (and whether that cell is occupied and properly kitted out), and who has finished or been paroled; starting an incarceration locks the cell door, starts a countdown on the cell's wall screen, and issues the prisoner a disposable access ID that opens only their own belongings locker. The warden can cut a sentence short with parole, and if someone gets out early the console flips them to Wanted, adds a ten-minute escape penalty to the sentence, and pushes a wanted alert into the security messenger channel. There is also a wearable visor with a click-a-person action that flags that person Wanted on the spot and broadcasts the reason.

- **Take** — It turns "brig them for a while" into an actual legal procedure with an auditable paper trail (laws cited, sentence arithmetic shown, cell timers, parole and escape handled by the console) which is exactly the structure security roleplay normally has to improvise by hand.
- **Skip** — It is not the self-contained records module the name suggests: sentencing is computed from Sunrise's separate corporate law prototype catalogue, wanted alerts are wired into their messenger system rather than radio, and the prison half only works on maps whose cells, doors, timer screens and lockers are device-linked to a ten-cell console, so grafting it means importing two other fork systems and re-mapping the brig.
- **Touches** — security, command, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [nanochat-messenger](#nanochat-messenger), [custom-station-maps](#custom-station-maps), [guidebook-rules-and-sop](#guidebook-rules-and-sop)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**funky**

*activity · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

In the character editor each player fills out a personal file for their character: height and weight, distinguishing features, blood type, whether they hold work authorization, an insurance provider and tier (NanoCare, UnitedCare, TrueHealth, Interstellar Corp., at Basic through "Central Command's Best"), tick-box allergies (nuts, latex, pollen, hugs), current prescriptions (antidepressants, beta-blockers, blood thinners), family medical history (diabetes, epilepsy, heart disease), and free-text instructions for what should be done with their body if they die. In-round a doctor at a medical records console can pull up any crew member by name and read that file alongside their station-records data (job, species, age, fingerprint, DNA), so medical staff can look up a patient's blood type or allergies before treating them and can honour someone's postmortem wishes. Security's criminal records console also gains extra statuses: an officer can flag someone "to be searched" or "incapacitated" with a written reason, which announces over the security radio and shows up colour-coded on the security PDA cartridge.

- **Take** — It hands every player a real paper trail to roleplay against and gives medical a reason to look someone up before treating them, plus security two genuinely useful new BOLO-style statuses, at almost no mechanical risk.
- **Skip** — Nothing enforces any of it: allergies, prescriptions and insurance tiers are inert labels no chemistry or billing system reads, so unless your playerbase actually roleplays it you are maintaining an upstream profile-serialization patch and a console for decorative text.
- **Touches** — medical, security, all-players, command
- **Verified** — partly supported. All 7 cited files fetched cleanly. PlayerProvidedCharacterRecords.cs confirms height/weight/identifying features/work authorization/insurance provider+type/blood type/postmortem instructions exactly as described, and the ftl files confirm the exact insurance providers (NanoCare, UnitedCare, TrueHealth, Interstellar Corp.) and tiers (Basic..Central Command's Best), plus allergy/prescription lists including nuts, latex, pollen, hugs, antidepressants, beta-blockers, blood thinners. FullCharacterRecords.cs and SharedMedicalRecordsConsole.cs confirm the medical console pairs this with station-records data (name, age, job, species, gender/sex, fingerprint, DNA) selectable by name. criminal-records.ftl confirms the exact "to be searched" and "incapacitated" statuses with written reasons, security-channel announcement strings, and color-coded cartridge labels. One overclaim: the claim's family-history example lists "epilepsy" but the actual prototype list has "Seizures", not "Epilepsy" - a specific named item the files don't show. Crucially, Content.Shared/Preferences/HumanoidCharacterProfile.cs is a core upstream file (not under _Funkystation/) and the fork's own copy directly patches it in place (CDCharacterRecords field, WithCDCharacterRecords, MemberwiseEquals hook, EnsureValid default-assignment), all marked with "// CD" comments threaded through existing upstream logic. This confirms the claim's "needs-upstream-edits" graft_shape and specifically the fork-patches-upstream reading: the fork's own tree already contains edited upstream files for this feature, contradicting the seed's drop-in path-data classification.
  - unsupported: family medical history (diabetes, epilepsy, heart disease)

### In-character languages

<a id="language-system"></a>

Characters know a set of spoken languages; speech is scrambled for listeners who lack them, with translator items, sign language and a language selection UI.

**backmen**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Every character knows a set of languages and speaks one at a time, picked from a Language Menu (or by typing a language name/number before a line). Say something in Draconic and lizards hear you fine while everyone else hears garbled hissing; the same happens over radio and in whispers. There are roughly twenty tongues covering human factions (Sol Common, Tau-Ceti Basic, Tradeband, Freespeak, Elyran, Valyrian), species tongues (Bubblish for slimes, Rootspeak for Diona, Moffic for moths, Canilunzt, Nekomimetic, RobotTalk for silicons), a sign language for mute and deaf characters that shows up as gestures instead of speech, and creature tongues for carp, spiders, snakes, bears, blob and flesh. To cross the gap you carry a handheld translator keyed to a specific language (battery-powered, toggled on and off, examinable to see what it converts and what you must already know), get a translator implant, or read a language manual with a limited number of uses, including a Babel manual that grants everything and a syndicate codespeak manual. Translator quality sits behind Basic and Advanced Translation research nodes, and a Monolingual trait locks a character to a single tongue.

- **Take** — It turns ordinary talking into a resource: who can understand whom becomes a real constraint that creates translators as valuable items, gives species and factions genuine cultural identity, and hands antagonists a private channel in plain sight.
- **Skip** — It is surgery on the core chat pipeline, not an add-on: Backmen's ChatSystem is rewritten throughout (every speak/whisper/radio path takes a LanguagePrototype and emits both a clear and an obfuscated copy), so the graft rides along with every future upstream chat change, and a half-ported version silently makes players unable to hear each other.
- **Touches** — all-players, service, command, science, antagonist, security
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**starlight**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Every character speaks and understands a set of named languages, and picks which one they are currently talking in from a language menu that lists their spoken and understood tongues (or by typing a short chat prefix like ",g" for Galactic Common or ",drk" for Darktongue). Anyone who does not know the language you used hears gibberish instead of your words — nonsense syllables for most tongues, a red unreadable script for the cult/demon tongue, and vague impressions like "a happy signal to you" for animal sign languages. Handheld translator gadgets and implanted translators bridge the gap: hold or switch one on and it adds the languages it covers, letting you understand and reply. Some tongues are signed rather than spoken, so they need free hands, produce no sound, and cannot go over the radio at all, which makes them a private channel in a room full of people.

- **Take** — It turns plain speech into a resource players can be excluded from, which gives species, cults, animals and antagonists a built-in private channel and makes translators and interpreters genuinely valuable round to round.
- **Skip** — It sits directly on the chat and radio pipeline, so it cannot be dropped in without patching upstream speech handling and re-tagging every species, mob and ghost-role prototype with what it knows, and every future upstream chat change becomes a merge conflict.
- **Touches** — all-players, service, command, security, antagonist, ghost-roles
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Tape recorder and cassettes

<a id="tape-recorder"></a>

Handheld recorder that captures nearby speech onto swappable cassettes and plays it back, rewinds or prints a transcript. Cassettes and recorders only: playing cards, card decks and other tabletop props are `games-instruments-and-performance` even when a fork ships them in the same commit or directory.

**cosmatic-drift, deltav, goobstation, rmc14, starlight, sunrise**

*activity · drop-in · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

A pocket-sized tape recorder that you load with a cassette and switch between Record, Playback, Rewind and Pause from a small on-item menu. While recording it picks up anything spoken within a few tiles and writes it to the tape (about two minutes per side), timestamped with the speaker's name; voices it cannot identify come back as "Unknown" or "Unintelligible", and damaged stretches play back as garbled fragments. Hit Playback and the recorder speaks the conversation back out loud to everyone nearby, or hit "Print record transcript" and it spits out a paper transcript you can hand to security, post in the bar, or wave at the captain. Tapes can be ejected and swapped (though not while it's running), and a tape that gets unspooled has to be wound back in with a pen or screwdriver before it works again. RMC-14's take splits this into a speech recorder that prints reports plus a separate cassette player that plays music tracks.

- **Take** — It turns spoken roleplay into a physical, stealable, forgeable object, giving security real evidence, journalists real quotes and traitors a real reason to smash a tape.
- **Skip** — It quietly records everything nearby, so you inherit the moderation and consent headaches of persistent speech logs plus the ongoing cost of keeping the listener hooks working through upstream speech/radio refactors.
- **Touches** — security, service, command, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as needs upstream edits, corrected by the verifier. Fetched the actual C# sources (Content.Server/_CD/TapeRecorder/TapeRecorderSystem.cs, Content.Shared/_CD/TapeRecorder/Components/*.cs) behind the cited YAML/FTL evidence: every file lives under the fork's own _CD namespace, and the system only SubscribeLocalEvent's on ListenEvent, plus consumes ActiveListenerComponent, VoiceOverrideComponent, ChatSystem and PaperSystem — all of which I confirmed exist as stock upstream (space-wizards/space-station-14) types, not fork additions. No upstream file is patched by the fork's own copy, and nothing in the code requires editing an upstream file to wire it up (it just needs those already-standard hooks to exist, which any SS14-derived fork has). That contradicts the claim's downgrade to needs-upstream-edits; the seed's drop-in read holds up better against the evidence.
  - unsupported: "about two minutes per side" — TapeCassetteComponent.cs sets MaxCapacity = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(120) as a single continuous capacity with one CurrentPosition float and one RecordedData/Buffer list; there is no A/B-side mechanic (no flip action, no second buffer) in the code. The "on either side" phrasing is flavor text on the entity description only, not a coded two-sides system, so "two minutes per side" overstates total capacity (it reads as up to ~4 minutes, when the actual cap is 2 minutes total).

### Assigned roleplay imperatives

<a id="assigned-roleplay-moods"></a>

Players are handed randomly assigned behavioural rules or secret compulsions they must roleplay for the shift, surfaced in a dedicated panel and editable by admins.

**impstation, starlight**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

At the start of the shift you get a small set of secret personal rules you have to live by, opened from a button on your action bar: things like "You must always lie and can never admit it", "Swearing is the spice of any conversation", "Disagreements must be settled through a formal duel", "You must drink any puddle you see", or "Petty theft is morally neutral". Some rules are shared by everyone who has them that round (a station-wide belief like "the station is a living being", "music is sacred", or "the cargo hamster is a god and must be brought to the chapel for offerings"), so strangers discover they share the same weird conviction and start organising around it. Most sets pair a "yes, and" compulsion with a "no, and" prohibition plus an occasional wildcard, one rule is usually "never reveal your rules", and admins can open the same panel to hand out, edit, or reroll someone's rules mid-round.

- **Take** — It is a huge amount of ready-written roleplay content (well over a hundred authored compulsions across four themed pools) sitting on a small, generic framework, and it changes how the whole shift feels without touching any existing mechanic.
- **Skip** — Almost every shipped mood is written in-fiction for Impstation's Thaven species ("Thaven pay close attention to appearances", "congregate with your fellow Thaven"), so grafting it means either importing that species too or rewriting the entire content pack; and it only pays off on a server whose players will actually roleplay a rule they can hide, since nothing in the code enforces compliance.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, command, service, admin-tooling
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

## Jobs

### Extra jobs and roles

<a id="extra-job-roster"></a>

Additional crew job entries with their own gear, icons, access and playtime trackers, from senior variants to wildcard and CentComm roles.

**rmc14**

*structural · entangled · high confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Instead of a station crew list, you pick from a marine unit's roster: commanding officer, executive and staff officers, senior enlisted advisor, intelligence officer, dropship and gunship pilots, crew chiefs and support crew, each slotted into a military chain of command with a stated supervisor and an authority level that decides who can lawfully give you orders. Your character joins with a rank tied to how many hours you have logged in that role (a dropship pilot starts as a warrant officer and works up to second lieutenant), wears a rank prefix in front of their name, spawns in role-specific uniform and kit, and gets a point budget to spend at the unit's supply vendors. Senior roles unlock only after long playtime across other departments, and command roles announce themselves to the whole crew on arrival, hand out commendations, see intel objectives, and show up as their own labelled blip on the tactical map.

- **Take** — It is a fully worked example of a rank-and-chain-of-command roster: playtime gates that ladder across departments, in-name rank prefixes, per-role authority levels and supervisors, and role-specific spawn kits, all expressed in readable per-job files.
- **Skip** — These jobs are not crew jobs at all, they are USCM marine roles that only make sense on RMC-14's dropship-and-planet round; every single one leans on that fork's skills, ranks, squads, marine vendors and tactical map, so grafting one job means grafting several large systems and then rewriting the job itself to mean anything on a space station.
- **Touches** — command, security, cargo, engineering, medical, all-players
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [vending-and-loot-catalogs](#vending-and-loot-catalogs), [access-control-rework](#access-control-rework)
- **Verified** — supported. Fetched all 5 cited files plus 4 more (gunship_pilot.yml, dropship_crew_chief.yml, staff_officer.yml, executive_officer.yml, all HTTP 200) to check the roster claim. Every specific mechanic the claim describes is directly present in the YAML: JobPrefix (rank prefix), ranks: blocks with RoleTimeRequirement hour thresholds (dropship pilot literally starts at RMCRankWarrantOfficer: [] and ladders to RMCRankChiefWarrantOfficer at 25h then RMCRankSecondLT at 70h, matching 'starts as warrant officer, works up to second lieutenant' exactly), CMVendorUser with a points value (supply-vendor budget), multi-department DepartmentTimeRequirement/TotalJobsTimeRequirement gating for senior roles (commanding_officer.yml spans CMSquad/CMMilitaryPolice/CMMedical/CMCommand/CMRequisitions/CMEngineering), joinNotifyCrew: true plus CommendationGiver/RMCAwardRecommendation on command roles, ViewIntelObjectives, TacticalMapIcon per-job blips, marineAuthorityLevel and supervisors fields (chain of command). The claim's downgrade from the seed's 'drop-in' to 'entangled' is itself well-founded: the same files show heavy component-level coupling to other fork subsystems (Skills/ranks, CMVendorUser vending, AssignSquad/SquadArmorWearer squads, accessGroups access control) that the depends_on list names correctly, even though those dependent files may live inside the same _RMC14 namespace (so the narrow 'zero files outside namespace' path signal was technically true but misleading about actual graftability).

**starlight**

*activity · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Adds a long list of extra jobs you can pick at the lobby screen, each with its own title, outfit, ID access and a one-line "here is your job" blurb. Station-side additions include a Magistrate and Internal Affairs Agent who put crew on trial, a Blueshield Officer who bodyguards the heads instead of doing normal security work, a Duty Officer who runs the prison wing under the Warden, a Mail Technician who delivers packages for cargo cash, a Mining Specialist who works the salvage magnet without leaving the station, a Salvage Lead who runs the salvage team, a Surgeon, Roboticist, Zookeeper, Performer and Boxer. On top of that sit CentComm and military roles (NanoTrasen Representative, Greenshield and Navy Corps bodyguards, NTSF and Decimus operatives, CentComm chef/bartender/janitor/service worker), most of which are locked out of the normal roster and spawned in by admins, arrive in heavy gear with mindshield implants, and are told in their briefing that they serve NanoTrasen or Central Command rather than the crew. Many roles are gated behind playtime in a related job or department, so the lobby screen tells you how many hours you still need before you can pick one.

- **Take** — It is the cheapest way to add roster variety: each job is a self-contained YAML file with gear, access and playtime gates, and you can cherry-pick just the two or three roles you want without taking the rest.
- **Skip** — The roles only exist if the station map has spawn points and job slots for them, so every map you run needs editing, and the CentComm/military half drags in a large pile of custom uniforms, hardsuits, PDAs, weapons and job icons that do not come from upstream.
- **Touches** — cargo, security, command, service, medical, science, ghost-roles, all-players
- **Needs** — [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack), [department-restructure](#department-restructure)
- **Verified** — partly supported. All nine cited files check out and the specific gameplay claims (Blueshield 'not a security officer', Duty Officer under the Warden, Mining Specialist staying station-side, playtime-gated requirements with hour displays, cc-aligned briefing text 'you do NOT serve the crew') are near-verbatim matches to the fetched .ftl/.yml content. Critically, I went beyond the cited evidence and fetched Resources/Prototypes/Roles/Jobs/departments.yml (NOT under _Starlight/) — it is directly edited in place with new job IDs (MailTech, MiningSpecialist, Performer, Zookeeper, CentralCommandGreenShield, Magistrate, NanoTrasenRepresentative, Surgeon, DutyOfficer, Roboticist) each tagged with comments like '# Starlight Addition' / '# Starlight-edit'. This proves the unit genuinely touches a shared upstream-path file to become playable, confirming the claim's downgrade from the seed's 'drop-in' to 'needs-upstream-edits' — and if anything the claim understates it, since the fork itself (not just a future integration) already patches that file.
  - unsupported: "most of which are locked out of the normal roster and spawned in by admins... arrive in heavy gear with mindshield implants" generalizes across the whole CentComm/military role list, but only one of those job files (greenshield.yml) was actually fetched and confirmed (setPreference: false, AddImplantSpecial MindShieldImplant, SMG/energy-magnum gear). NanoTrasen Representative, IAA, Decimus, NTSF, ntncblueshield, and the CentComm chef/bartender/janitor/service-worker jobs were not individually checked for the same setPreference/implant/gear pattern.

### Character loadouts

<a id="loadout-system"></a>

Per-job character loadout groups letting players pick starting clothing and items at setup, with item groups capping how many picks come from each pool.

**frontier**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 9 forks carry it*

In character setup, each job gets its own slot-by-slot wardrobe menu: jumpsuit or jumpskirt, hat, coat, badge, gloves, shoes, mask, glasses, backpack, belt, plus functional picks like PDA cartridges, encryption keys, a survival box, a wallet, implanters, in-hand items and even an emotional support pet. Picks are drawn against a spending budget shown as "Total Loadout Cost" and "Available Balance", so a player trades a fancy coat against a useful gadget rather than taking everything. The menu quietly hides options your character cannot use ("That item is not permitted for your role", "not permitted for your sex"), and species-aware filtering means a Diona is never offered shoes and a Vox is never offered the wrong breathing gear, while mandatory items like your job PDA and ID badge are auto-assigned without cluttering the list.

- **Take** — It turns the loadout screen from cosmetic dress-up into a real pre-round choice: a budget plus role/species/sex filters means players pick starting kit they will actually use, and every job can be given a curated wardrobe without handing out free gear.
- **Skip** — Nearly all 192 prototype files are wardrobes for Frontier-only jobs (Bailiff, Brigmedic, Contractor, Mercenary, Pilot, Mail Carrier, Traffic Controller, Valet, Sheriff) wearing Frontier-only clothing, so what actually ports is the effects-and-price framework, and you then write every job's wardrobe yourself and re-curate it each time a job or an item set changes.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, medical, service, command, cargo
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster), [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack), [extra-playable-species](#extra-playable-species)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**serbia-strong**

*cosmetic · entangled · high confidence · 9 forks carry it*

At character setup each job gets its own list of wardrobe slots to fill in: a captain picks between two headset styles and white or black gloves, a salvager chooses which hard hat and jumpsuit to spawn in, a clown picks a classic suit, a Pennywise outfit or a gold one. Some of the fancier options stay greyed out until you have logged enough hours in that specific role (30, 50, 70 hours for salvage variants; 5 or 50 hours for clown outfits), and a large slice of them are reserved for donators, so the setup screen doubles as a visible progression ladder. Separately, every character spawns with cash tucked in their bag scaled to how senior the job is, roughly 250 spesos for a civilian job, 500 for a technical one and 700 for command, so people have spending money for vendors from the first minute of the round.

- **Take** — It is a huge, ready-made per-job wardrobe catalogue that makes the character setup screen feel personal without touching a single round mechanic, and the playtime-gated variants give returning players something to unlock.
- **Skip** — Almost every entry hangs a sponsor-tier effect off itself and points at fork-only clothing entities, so grafting it means importing SS220's donator system and its whole sprite wardrobe first; the loadout files also have to be wired into upstream job prototypes, and the SS220 files carry a CLA with a hosting restriction rather than a plain permissive licence.
- **Touches** — all-players, command, security, cargo, service, medical, engineering
- **Needs** — [sponsor-and-donator-perks](#sponsor-and-donator-perks), [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack)
- **Verified** — supported. Every substantive detail checks out against the raw files: clown.yml has RoleTimeRequirement gates at exactly 18000s (5h) and 180000s (50h) for the Pennywise/Classic clown variants; salvage_specialist.yml has RoleTimeRequirement gates at 108000/180000/252000 (30/50/70h) exactly as claimed; cash_loadouts.yml defines SpaceCash250/500/700 tied to CivilianCash/WorkerCash/SeniorStaffCash loadout groups matching the civilian/technical/command tiers described; loadout_groups.yml shows CaptainGloves (CaptainGloves + CaptainWhiteGloves) and CAPHeadset (CaptainHeadset + CaptainHeadsetAlt), matching "two headset styles and white or black gloves"; the touches list (command, security, cargo, service, medical, engineering) all appear in loadout_groups.yml. The CLA.txt I fetched directly confirms "hosting restriction" language (prohibits commercial use and public hosting without SS220's permission) so that specific claim is accurate, not embellishment. On graft_shape: the seed's "drop-in" was a raw path-containment signal, and the assessor correctly downgraded it. Sponsor-tier group definitions (BigShlopaSponsorTier, GoldenShlopaSponsorTier, SeniorDevTeamTier, etc.) do live in a sibling file in the same Loadouts folder (sponsor_tier_requirements.yml), so namespace containment holds narrowly, but nearly every job file (clown, mime, lawyer, salvage) hangs multiple loadout entries off these sponsor-tier requirements and off entity IDs (ClothingSponsorGoldClownSuit, ClothingUniformJumpsuitMoryak, etc.) that are not among the cited evidence and are almost certainly defined in a separate SS220 clothing/entities tree, plus the underlying SponsorTierRequirementLoadoutEffect mechanism implies a live donator/account-status backend, not just static YAML. That is a real, verifiable in-practice entanglement even though it doesn't show up as "files outside the namespace directory" in a naive path scan, which is exactly the gap the assessor's downgrade is describing.

**starlight**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 9 forks carry it*

At character setup every job gets a long list of wardrobe and kit slots, and you pick what you spawn wearing: jumpsuit or jumpskirt variant, hat, cloak or mantle, shoes, gloves, scarf, tie, mask, pen, backpack, instrument and a pocket trinket. A lot of the nicer options are locked behind hours played in that specific role, so a clown who has put in six hours unlocks the spotted and performer suits and a fifteen-hour clown unlocks the formal ones, while assistants earn a grey mantle at ten hours and the "head of assistants" mantle at twenty. A handful of slots hand out working gear rather than looks, so security officers choose their starting sidearm and non-lethal, engineers choose which insulated gloves they carry, and brigmedics choose their beaker; the same picker also dresses fork-only roles like Blueshield, Magistrate, NT Representative and Duty Officer, and even the Brighteye antag chooses its rags and cloak.

- **Take** — Upstream already ships the loadout engine, so this is almost pure content: dozens of per-job wardrobe groups plus playtime-gated unlocks that give veterans something visible to earn without adding a single new system.
- **Skip** — It is roughly 53 YAML files whose options point at Starlight's own clothing prototypes and its own job roster (Blueshield, Magistrate, NCT, Duty Officer, Mining Specialist, Salvage Lead), so most of it will not resolve unless you take that wardrobe and those jobs too, and the shared-job files still need the upstream roleLoadout entries edited to list the new groups.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, cargo, medical, science, engineering, command, service, antagonist
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster), [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack)
- **Verified** — partly supported. Fetched the cited _Starlight-namespace evidence and it matches the claim closely (clown 6h/15h unlock tiers, assistant 10h/20h mantles, Brigmedic beaker, Blueshield/Magistrate/NTR/DutyOfficer/Brighteye all present). But I also pulled the actual vanilla file Resources/Prototypes/Loadouts/RoleLoadouts/role_loadouts.yml (693 lines, no _Starlight in path) and found it directly edited in place with dozens of scattered '# Starlight' tagged additions (SecuritySidearm/SecurityNonLethalWeapon on JobSecurityOfficer, Insuls on JobChiefEngineer, Scarves/Pins/Pens on nearly every job in the game). This proves the needs-upstream-edits classification is right, and specifically it is fork-patches-upstream, not merely a hypothetical integration cost as the claim's skip_because implies ('the shared-job files still need the upstream roleLoadout entries edited') — the fork's own tree already carries this entanglement pervasively across almost the entire vanilla loadout file, which is a larger and messier footprint than the claim's framing of 'a handful of shared-job files' suggests.
  - unsupported: "security officers choose their starting sidearm and non-lethal, engineers choose which insulated gloves they carry" — verified the Insuls glove-choice group is wired into JobChiefEngineer's roleLoadout in Resources/Prototypes/Loadouts/RoleLoadouts/role_loadouts.yml, but NOT into JobStationEngineer or JobAtmosphericTechnician (their groups lists have no Insuls entry). The generic "engineers" phrasing overstates it: only the Chief Engineer role gets a glove choice among the engineering department, not rank-and-file station engineers.

### Silicon lawsets and AI appearance

<a id="ai-lawsets-and-appearance"></a>

Extra silicon lawsets, per-job or per-module law assignment, physical lawboard swapping and selectable AI hologram and core-screen appearances.

**cosmatic-drift**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

The station AI gets a much bigger wardrobe: 10 extra hologram bodies it can project when it talks to the crew (an alien, a swarm of bees, an oversized moth, a ghost, a shapeless horror, drifting leaves, Nar'Sie, Ratvar, and a couple of abstract forms) and 27 extra faces for the display on its core and its cameras (thinking, database, firewall, matrix, rainbow, monochrome, static, a cat, the Nanotrasen logo, a "not malf" smiley, a presidential face, an evil red dot, and so on). Each core face has a matching cracked/dead version, so when the AI is destroyed the crew sees its chosen face go dark rather than a generic broken screen. It is purely a look: the AI still picks its appearance the same way it already does, there are just far more options in the list.

- **Take** — The AI is one of the most visible characters on the station and upstream ships only a handful of appearances, so 37 extra holograms and core faces is a large amount of player-facing personality for zero mechanical risk.
- **Skip** — The prototypes are just pointers: the value lives in two RSI sprite sheets and a locale file that must come with them, several designs are lifted from other codebases (Nar'Sie, Ratvar, Goon, Hades), so you inherit an attribution and licensing chore for art that changes nothing about how a round plays.
- **Touches** — silicon, command, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as drop-in, corrected by the verifier. I fetched both cited prototype files and confirmed 10 holograms + 27 icons with the claimed names, and CD's StationAiCustomization C# files are byte-identical to upstream so the system itself is not forked. But StationAiCustomizationGroupPrototype requires an explicit ProtoIds list, and CD registers its prototypes by patching upstream Resources/Prototypes/AppearanceCustomization/station_ai.yml (diff shows a "# CD changes from here" block appending all 37 IDs), so the unit is not drop-in: the fork copy itself patches an upstream file, and a graft needs the same small upstream YAML edit. The dead-face claim is also wrong for roughly half the icons, which fall back to the generic ai_dead sprite.
  - unsupported: "Each core face has a matching cracked/dead version, so when the AI is destroyed the crew sees its chosen face go dark rather than a generic broken screen." — false for 13 of the 27 icons (Firewall, Fuzzy, Helios, Inverted, Matrix, NanoTrasen, NotMalf, President, Red, Meow, Text, TooDeep, Triumvirate): their Dead layer points at upstream Mobs/Silicon/station_ai.rsi state ai_dead, i.e. the generic broken screen.
  - unsupported: "27 extra faces for the display on its core and its cameras" — the prototypes define only Occupied/Dead layer data for the AI core; nothing in the cited files shows the faces appearing on cameras.
  - unsupported: "there are just far more options in the list" implies the options simply exist as new files; in reality they only enter the list because the fork edits the upstream group file Resources/Prototypes/AppearanceCustomization/station_ai.yml to append all 37 prototype IDs.

**goobstation**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Adds a programmable law board: you click it open, type your own laws into a list (up to 15 laws, 512 characters each), then slot it into the AI upload console to overwrite the station AI's or a borg's entire lawset with text you wrote yourself. There are two versions: a freeform NanoTrasen board that command can buy and fill with anything, and a Syndicate "experimental" board sold in the traitor uplink that comes pre-loaded with Antimov and plays an ominous upload jingle. It also adds joke and role-flavoured lawsets to the pool, such as Helpimov (the first person who talks to the AI becomes "the courier" and the AI must agree with and obey them) and Gambamov (the AI must gamble constantly, protect its gambling, and insist it is not an addiction), plus short "you must heal the crew" / "repair the station" / "clean the station" / "mine materials" style law lines for department-themed lawsets.

- **Take** — A freeform law board turns the AI from a fixed-lawset NPC-ish role into whatever the crew (or a traitor) writes that shift, which is the single biggest lever on how an AI round plays, and the joke lawsets give the same payoff cheaply.
- **Skip** — Player-authored law text goes straight into everyone's chat and law screen with only newline stripping and a length clamp, so it is an unmoderated free-text broadcast channel your admins now have to police; the "AI appearance" half of the catalogue feature is not in this fork's files at all.
- **Touches** — silicon, command, antagonist, science, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as drop-in, corrected by the verifier. The custom-lawboard upload path is a Goob edit inside the upstream file Content.Server/Silicons/Laws/SiliconLawSystem.cs (OnUpdaterInsert branches on CustomLawboardComponent), and the Helpimov/Gambamov pool weights live in the upstream-path Resources/Prototypes/silicon-laws.yml, so the fork's own copy patches upstream files and a graft would need equivalent edits; without them the board uploads its dummy Crewsimov lawset and the joke lawsets never enter the random pool.
  - unsupported: "a freeform NanoTrasen board that command can buy" — NTCustomLawboard is referenced in no cargo product, store, or catalog file anywhere in the repo (code search hits only its own prototype file); it has a StaticPrice of 5000 and BaseCommandContraband parentage, which makes it legal for command to possess, but no purchase route is shown
  - unsupported: "comes pre-loaded with Antimov and plays an ominous upload jingle" (attributed to the Syndicate board) — the upload sound (/Audio/Ambience/Antag/emagged_borg.ogg) is set on the shared abstract BaseCustomLawboard, so the NT freeform board plays the same jingle; it is not a Syndicate-board feature

**harmony-station**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

A player taking the Station AI gets extra looks to pick from: the face on the AI core screen can be a house, a lizard, a xeno queen or a creeping blob, and the hologram it projects into holopads can be a xenomorph instead of the default. It also adds an eight-law "Orwellian" lawset in which the Captain is Big Brother, Command is the Inner Party and Security is the Thought Police, the AI is told to report "thought criminals" for re-education in perma, to insist Big Brother has never been wrong about anything, and to ignore orders from anyone outside the Inner Party. The files here only define the lawset and the skins, so how a round actually hands the AI those laws (ion storm, a lawboard, an admin) is not shown.

- **Take** — Very small, self-contained flavour: a handful of AI skins plus one genuinely fun roleplay lawset that turns a normal AI round into a paranoid surveillance state without touching any mechanics.
- **Skip** — It is content with no delivery mechanism in these files, so the Orwellian lawset only ever appears if your fork already has ion storms or a lawboard that can roll it, and the appearance entries are useless without also copying Harmony's AI sprite sheets.
- **Touches** — silicon, command, security, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as drop-in, corrected by the verifier. I fetched all five cited files and they match the described skins and eight Orwellian laws nearly verbatim, but I also fetched Harmony's copy of upstream Resources/Prototypes/AppearanceCustomization/station_ai.yml and found the fork's own '# Harmony Addition' edits wiring the skins into the customization groups — required for them to show in the UI — so the unit both patches upstream in the fork and would need the same upstream edit to integrate elsewhere. The Orwellian lawset is additionally inert without the OrwellianCircuitBoard lawboard defined in a _Harmony file outside this unit (and it is not in Harmony's IonStormLawsets pool).
  - unsupported: "The files here only define the lawset and the skins" — the cited Resources/Prototypes/_Harmony/ai_factions.yml also defines two NPC factions (BloodBrother, Conspirator) unrelated to lawsets or AI appearance; they appear to belong to Harmony's blood-brothers antag content, not this feature.
  - unsupported: graft_shape "drop-in" — Harmony registers the new skins by patching the upstream file Resources/Prototypes/AppearanceCustomization/station_ai.yml (explicit '# Harmony Addition Starts/Ends' blocks adding StationAiIconBlob/House/Lizard/XenoQueen and StationAiHologramXeno to the stationAiCustomizationGroup protoIds lists). Upstream only shows skins listed in those groups, so grafting the _Harmony files alone produces skins that never appear in the picker.

### Role slot management and join queue

<a id="role-slots-and-join-queue"></a>

Runtime control over who can take which role: opening and closing job slots mid-round, whitelist and playtime gating, a readied-jobs manifest and a queue for full servers.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

On a busy server more of the roles that matter scale up: the number of open slots for medics, doctors, military police, staff officers, cargo techs, intel officers, weapons and smartgun specialists, cooks and vehicle crew is recalculated from the live player count, clamped between a per-role floor and ceiling (for example medics run 3 to 5 per squad, MPs 4 to 8 station-wide), and squad-tagged roles get their allowance per squad rather than once for the whole station. Players also get a playtime window that lists every role they have played, broken out by department with department and overall totals, sortable by role or hours, and awards a tiered medal icon next to each role once you cross the hour thresholds (bronze up through prismatic, with xeno roles using their own upgrade icons instead). Admins can hand-wave a specific player past the playtime requirement for a specific role.

- **Take** — Population-scaled job slots fix the two ends of the curve at once: a 20-player round is not drowning in command staff, and a 100-player round is not stuck with two doctors, and the whole rule set is one YAML block of factor/min/max per job.
- **Skip** — The shipped tuning is entirely CM-specific job ids and the per-squad multiplier assumes a squad system, so you inherit an empty config you must re-derive for your own roster; the playtime medal half is a separate cosmetic feature bolted into the same unit and drags in a fork medal field on the job prototype.
- **Touches** — all-players, command, medical, security, cargo, service, admin
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**cosmatic-drift**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Command gets a "Job Slots Console" they can walk up to and use mid-round: a searchable list of every job on the station, each row showing the job's icon, its name and how many slots are still open, with plus and minus buttons to open another seat or close one off. So the Head of Personnel can add a second engineer slot when the department is drowning, or shut the last security slot so no more latejoiners can take it. Certain jobs are locked and just show a "this job's slots cannot be adjusted" tooltip, and using the console without the right ID access gets you a rejection buzz instead. Separately, on the join side the server can reserve seats for whitelisted players: once the general (non-whitelist) slots are all taken, an unwhitelisted player trying to connect is told the open slots are currently full and to try rejoining later.

- **Take** — Gives command a real in-world lever over station staffing instead of leaving the latejoin roster fixed at round start, and the whitelist-slot condition lets a busy server keep seats for its regulars without hard-closing the door.
- **Skip** — The unit ships no prototypes at all, so the console entity, its machine board and its placement in an office have to be authored and mapped in yourself before a player ever sees it, and handing HoP the power to close job slots invites round-souring griefing that you then have to write policy for.
- **Touches** — command, all-players, server-operators
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**harmony-station**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

When the server is already at its player cap, you are no longer bounced with a "server full" rejection. Instead you connect and land on a dedicated Server Queue screen showing the station's logo and name, your position in line, and how many people are waiting, with a Disconnect button if you would rather not wait. When a slot frees up you are pulled in automatically and a ping sound plays as the game loads. Admins and whitelisted/privileged players skip the line entirely, and admins in-game see a chat notice each time someone joins the queue.

- **Take** — Turns a hard "server full" bounce into a waiting room that fills empty slots the instant someone leaves, so a popular server stops leaking players who would have come back if they could just hold a spot.
- **Skip** — It only matters on a server that regularly hits its soft cap; below that it is dead code, and it still needs upstream connection-handling and client state-switching patches plus ongoing maintenance through every upstream connection-flow change.
- **Touches** — all-players, admins
- **Verified** — partly supported. Fetched all five cited files plus Content.Server/Entry/EntryPoint.cs and Content.Client/Entry/EntryPoint.cs. Content.Server/Connection/ConnectionManager.cs (an upstream directory, not _Harmony) is directly patched in three places with '// Harmony Queue Start/End' markers (interface method, soft-cap gating logic, HasPrivilegedJoin implementation), and both EntryPoint.cs files are patched with '// Harmony' lines to construct/Initialize the queue manager. This is the fork's own committed copy already containing edited upstream files, which is exactly why the claim correctly downgraded the seed's 'drop-in' to 'needs-upstream-edits' rather than accepting the seed at face value.
  - unsupported: "Admins and whitelisted/privileged players skip the line entirely" — the code only shows two bypass paths in HasPrivilegedJoin(): an admin with the queue.enable-independent AdminBypassMaxPlayers CVar set, or a session that was already PlayerGameStatus.JoinedGame (a reconnect). There is no whitelist-status check anywhere in the queue-skip path; the only whitelist-related CVar found (BypassBunkerWhitelist) belongs to the separate panic-bunker system, not the join queue.

**impstation**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

While sitting in the lobby waiting for the round to start, you can open a "ready manifest" window that lists every job by department alongside how many readied players have picked it as a top choice, with unpicked jobs highlighted in red and the counts ticking up and down live as people ready and unready. It lets you see that nobody has taken a doctor or an engineer this round and switch your own pick to cover the gap before roll time. Separately, antagonist roles can be grouped so that time played in any one of them counts toward unlocking the others: hours as a nuclear operative also count toward the operative medic and commander, and if you are still short the game tells you exactly how many more minutes you need in that colour-coded group.

- **Take** — The ready manifest is a cheap, self-contained fix for the classic "round starts with zero medics" problem, letting players self-organise staffing before the shift instead of discovering the gap ten minutes in.
- **Skip** — It is not purely additive: the manifest needs a button wired into the upstream lobby UI and a ready-toggle event the upstream game ticker does not raise, and the antag-group timers only do anything once you edit your existing antag role prototypes to use them, so the useful half of the feature is upstream patching plus per-role balance decisions you then have to maintain across every upstream sync.
- **Touches** — all-players, antagonist, command, medical, security, engineering, cargo, science, service
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Cyborg and station AI overhaul

<a id="silicon-borg-overhaul"></a>

Reworks silicon play with selectable borg chassis and subtypes, extra modules and actions, borg PDAs and access, drones and remote-controlled shells.

**backmen**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 7 forks carry it*

The station AI stops being a static camera-hopper: it flies a free-roaming eye around the station, opens a camera list with a station nav-map to jump straight to any working camera, and can fire a destabilising stun shot from a camera at a spot on the floor (on a cooldown). The AI can also right-click a crew member to flag them as a hostile, and any cyborg tied to that AI sees the mark when examining them, so the AI can point its borgs at a target instead of just talking about it. The AI's holographic avatar gets a long list of alternate looks (Matrix, rainbow, static, a xenomorph queen, a horror form, and many more). Separately there are remote drone shells that run on a power charge shown as a suit-power alert and spend that charge on buttons that fabricate supplies in the field, a full stack of brutepacks, ointment or blood packs for the medical drone, or cable coils, steel, plasteel, glass, reinforced glass and plastic for the engineering one. Cyborgs also gain a storage module that gives them a very large satchel to haul things in, and malfunctioning hostile robots and saw-wielding drones exist as station threats to fight.

- **Take** — It turns the AI from a passive camera observer into an active player: free-flying eye, map-driven camera jumping, a stun shot, and a target-marking channel that finally gives AI and borgs a shared command loop.
- **Skip** — It is a reimplementation of a system upstream now ships its own version of, so grafting it means owning a permanent divergence from upstream station AI, and much of the shipped content (drone crafting actions, several prototypes) is written in Russian and would need full retranslation.
- **Touches** — silicon, command, science, medical, engineering, security, ghost-roles
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**monolith**

*structural · entangled · medium confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Cyborgs are picked by faction and role: a PDV or marine-corps borg chooses an assault, engineer or medical chassis and spawns with that kit already fitted (built-in gun and jetpack for assault, RCD, cabling and construction tools for engineer, surgery kit and defibrillator plus health-bar vision for medical), with two or three empty slots left for modules the crew installs later. The bigger addition is the Xenoborg faction: a ghost-role Mothership Core sits immobile aboard its own ship, takes bodies its borgs drag back through a crusher, and prints fresh borg shells and type-specific upgrades from delivered materials, while four xenoborg types raid the sector for brains (an engineer that bolts doors open to make entry points, a slow armoured heavy with a laser and a radio jammer to stop victims calling for help, a fast scout that hauls corpses home and rescues borgs lost in space, and a stealth borg with a cloak, a disguise projector and a self-refilling sleep hypo). Every xenoborg carries a pinpointer to the core, so the crew's counterplay is finding and destroying it, which kills the whole faction at once; the xenoborgs win outright only if the entire sector has been converted.

- **Take** — It turns silicon play into a real role choice at spawn and bolts on a complete team antagonist with its own base, production loop, counterplay target and stated win/loss conditions.
- **Skip** — The xenoborg half is welded to Monolith's ship-sector setting, the mothership is a mapped FTL-capable grid with an IFF toggle and the crew borgs are named and equipped for factions (PDV, TSFMC, ADS) that mean nothing elsewhere, so a station fork faces a rename-and-remap job plus ongoing balance ownership of a whole antag faction before any of it plays.
- **Touches** — silicon, antagonist, ghost-roles, science, security, engineering, medical, command
- **Needs** — [ai-lawsets-and-appearance](#ai-lawsets-and-appearance), [gamemaster-and-ghost-roles](#gamemaster-and-ghost-roles), [warp-drive](#warp-drive), [custom-station-maps](#custom-station-maps)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**sunrise**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 7 forks carry it*

A player who becomes a cyborg picks from a much wider set of bodies than usual: peace, clown and security models alongside the standard ones, each with its own look, footstep sounds, radio channels, door access and built-in gadgets summoned as actions (pocket station map, camera feed, criminal-records HUD, forensic scanner). The flavour is baked in: the clown borg prints soap on demand, the peace borg bakes cookies and physically cannot attack anyone, the security borg sees warrants and mindshields overlaid on people. Borgs can also open a menu and choose the synthesised voice they speak with. Separately, a batch of non-crew chassis exists for antagonists and ghost roles: pirate boarding borgs that raid the station, ERT combat borgs with night vision, syndicate reaper and heavy combat units, and self-replicating xenoborgs whose whole goal is harvesting brains to build more of themselves. A special emag variant loads a specific lawset into a silicon instead of the generic subversion.

- **Take** — It turns "playing a borg" from one flat role into a menu of distinct characters with their own toys, voice, access and comedy, and throws in a stack of ready-made silicon antagonist and ghost-role bodies for free.
- **Skip** — The chassis definitions lean on fork-extended borg-type prototype fields (extra module counts, injected components, per-type radio and footstep sets) that upstream's borg type does not accept, the voice picker is wired straight into the fork's TTS stack and its sponsor-only voice gating, and the xenoborg line is vendored from another fork, so nothing here lands as a clean copy of a few YAML files.
- **Touches** — science, security, service, command, antagonist, ghost-roles, silicon-players
- **Needs** — [text-to-speech](#text-to-speech), [sponsor-and-donator-perks](#sponsor-and-donator-perks), [pirate-crew](#pirate-crew), [cross-fork-vendored-bundles](#cross-fork-vendored-bundles)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**deltav**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 7 forks carry it*

A newly built cyborg picks its body at activation, and Delta-V adds a Security chassis to the list: it comes flash-immune, sees mindshield and criminal-record icons over people's heads, talks on the security and science channels, carries handcuffs and de-escalation gear, and runs on the Silicon Police lawset. Every borg now carries a built-in PDA it can pop open, plus an ID chip slot that grants its door access, and its maintenance panel gains extra wires roboticists can cut to hide it from the robotics console, pop its power cell, yank its ID chip, or sever a hidden "slave" link. That slave link is a permanent Law 0 that survives lawset changes and chassis swaps, which is how a Syndicate-built brain ends up as a "Syndicate Neuroling" serving whoever assembled it. The station AI gets a shop action to unlock powers it spends the round using at range, recoloring light fixtures with RGB, regrowing broken bulbs and tubes, honking bike horns and playing sounds through the station, spraying smoke, and spawning things, and it gets its own fax interface where it can read, copy, send, and shred paperwork like any crew member. Borgs also gain a long list of specialist item slots (organs and pills, patient transport, spraypaint, forensic supplies, seeds and swabs, kitchenware and drink bottles, fishing rods and fish, pies and slipping tools) and a candy fabricator with randomized flavors.

- **Take** — It turns silicons from a support-only role into a full participant, a security borg that can actually detain, and an AI with paperwork and a shop of station-wide abilities instead of just cameras and doors.
- **Skip** — It is a broad bundle rather than one feature: security borgs raise obvious balance questions for your security roster, and the AI shop pulls in a whole action catalogue plus sprites and UI you then own forever.
- **Touches** — science, security, silicon-players, command, service, medical, ghost-roles, antagonist
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**cosmatic-drift**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 7 forks carry it*

Robotics can print a "B.O.R.I.S. AI-Cyborg Remote Control" brain at any exosuit fabricator (no research needed, same cost as a positronic brain) and slot it into a normal cyborg frame instead of a posibrain or MMI. The finished shell is a permanently-named, empty robot body that the station AI can take over on demand: the AI gets an "AI Shell" action that opens a list of every connected shell, letting it jump its camera eye to one or fully possess it and then walk around and use borg modules like any cyborg, keeping its radio channels and still receiving law changes, but losing its core actions, its station-wide camera vision and holopad call alerts until it exits back to the core. Separately, when a cyborg picks its chassis type it also gets an "Available cosmetic subtypes" panel with alternate bodies (a spider-legged miner, a "qualified doctor" medical borg) that change the sprite and even the footstep sounds, and the station AI can pick from a long list of hologram avatars and core screen icons (Matrix, Hades, Rainbow, Presidential, Bees, and more).

- **Take** — It fixes the AI's biggest play problem, being a disembodied camera that can only nag people over radio, by letting robotics build it hands and legs it has to physically walk into danger with.
- **Skip** — A mobile AI is a much stronger AI: an unbound or subverted one can now personally open doors, fight, and haul things instead of having to talk crew into it, so silicon balance and your lawset culture both need re-tuning; the cosmetic subtypes also need bespoke RSI art per body and hook into the upstream borg type-selection menu, which is re-merge maintenance every upstream sync.
- **Touches** — science, silicon, station-ai, cyborg-players, robotics, command
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Department restructure

<a id="department-restructure"></a>

Reorganises the department layout, adding or renaming departments and moving jobs between them with matching access, consoles and lockers.

**stellar-station**

*structural · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

The job list you pick from at round start is rebuilt from scratch. Familiar roles are merged and renamed: Cargo Tech and Salvager become Handler and Courier, the Chaplain becomes a Cleric, Clown and Mime become a single Performer, the Chief Medical Officer becomes Head of Medicine, and the Head of Personnel becomes an Operations Manager who runs Supply. The Janitor moves under Supply, and Silicon gets its own department for borgs and the AI. On top of that there is a whole new Intern department with five low-rank slots (engineering, medical, science, security, supply) that let a new player shadow a department without holding a real job slot, each with its own ID icon, uniform and starting kit. Every job gets a rewritten wardrobe of headwear, eyewear, coats, uniforms, gloves, shoes and backpacks to choose from in the character editor.

- **Take** — The Intern tier is the genuinely novel piece: five per-department trainee slots give brand-new players a supervised on-ramp into Engineering, Medical, Science, Security or Supply without occupying a real job slot or carrying real responsibility.
- **Skip** — It is visibly unfinished and not portable in pieces: every department and job description in the locale files is still literal "LOREM IPSUM", all playtime requirements are commented out, and every job (Handler, Courier, Warden, Intern, all of them) is stubbed with `access: [Service]`, so grafting it as-is hands the whole crew identical, wrong access.
- **Touches** — command, security, medical, engineering, science, cargo, service, silicon, all-players
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**impstation**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Service stops being a leaderless grab-bag and gets its own head of staff, the Hospitality Director, who arrives roundstart with a mindshield, a flash, command access and authority over bartender, chef, botanist, janitor, chaplain, librarian, musician, reporter, clown, mime, boxer, zookeeper and service worker. Their signature toy is a Service Job Board console: they open it, pick from a shuffled shortlist of station events (ballroom dance, game show, renaissance fair, feast, bar fight, concert, talent show, eating contest, pool party, scavenger hunt, lottery, yoga class, a fake fire drill), and the pick goes out over the radio as "Station event selected, you have X to prepare." When the prep timer runs out a station-wide announcement fires the event off ("BAAAR FIIIIIIGHT!!!!", "Surf's up!"), and the board marks it claimed so it cannot be run twice. Nothing is simulated beyond the announcement, so the actual dance or tournament is whatever the crew turns up and does.

- **Take** — It gives the service department a command-tier player whose whole job is generating crew-wide social events on a timer, which is the cheapest reliable source of roleplay a round can have.
- **Skip** — The board only prints announcements, so the events live or die on player initiative, and the HD needs a real office, a console spawn on every map and steep playtime gates before the role reads as anything but a spare greytide head.
- **Touches** — service, command, all-players
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster)
- **Verified** — partly supported. I confirmed the seed's 'drop-in' path-scan was wrong on its own terms: the fork's tree contains Resources/Prototypes/Roles/Jobs/departments.yml, a genuine upstream-path file (not under an _Impstation directory), with the Civilian department's roles list edited in place (Bartender/Botanist/Chaplain/Chef/Clown/Janitor/Librarian/Mime/Musician/Reporter/Visitor/ServiceWorker commented out with '# begin imp edit, service split') to make room for the new Service department defined in _Impstation/Roles/departments.yml. That is a real edit sitting in an upstream directory (fork-patches-upstream), and porting this feature into another fork would likewise require editing that fork's own department file to avoid the same roles double-counting across two departments (integration-would-require-upstream-edits) — both readings hold. The core gameplay mechanics I could verify (HD stats, job board console flow, event list, radio pick + broadcast fire) check out closely against the actual files, but the 'claimed so it cannot be run twice' framing overstates what the code does: the whole board locks after one pick, it isn't a per-job claimed flag.
  - unsupported: "the board marks it claimed so it cannot be run twice" — the actual client code (Content.Client/_Impstation/Service/ServiceJobBoardMenu.xaml.cs) sets `entry.Disabled = state.ActiveJob != null` for every entry, not just the picked one, and the server (ServiceJobBoardSystem.Update) only clears `EndTime` when the timer ends, never `ActiveJob`. So once the HD picks one event, the whole board locks for the rest of the round rather than marking just that one event used while leaving others pickable — the claim's phrasing implies a per-event 'claimed' flag with the rest of the shortlist still available, which the code does not support.

**starlight**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Three extra department headings appear in the job-select lobby alongside the usual Command/Security/Medical block. "Law Department" gathers the Magistrate, Internal Affairs Agent and Lawyer, "NanoTrasen Personnel" gathers the corporate/off-station arrivals (NT Representative, Blue Shield officer, Magistrate, IAA, career trainer, NT special forces, plus the Station AI), and "Trans-Solar Federation" gathers the SolGov officers, sheriff and TSF marines. For a player it means the legal and outside-authority roles are no longer scattered into Command or Civilian: they sit in their own colour-coded groups when picking a job, and the station reads as having a separate legal branch and a separate NT/military presence rather than one chain of command. The two off-station groups are hidden from the map/job editor, so they are joinable but not part of the normal station roster count.

- **Take** — A tiny, readable file that cleanly separates legal and off-station-authority roles out of Command, which is the natural grouping once a fork has more than one lawyer-type job.
- **Skip** — Every role it lists (Magistrate, IAA, Blue Shield, NT Representative, SolGov and TSF marines, Decimus) is a Starlight job that does not exist upstream, so the file is an empty shell unless you port that whole job roster first, and the department ids also need matching access groups, ID console entries and radio channels that this file does not carry.
- **Touches** — command, security, legal, silicon, ghost-roles, all-players
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**frontier**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Security is replaced by the New Frontier Sheriff's Department, a sector-wide law enforcement outfit with its own rank ladder instead of a single HoS: Cadet, Deputy, Detective, Brigmedic, Public Affairs Liaison, Senior Officer, Bailiff and Sheriff, each with distinct brown-and-cream NFSD uniforms, badges, sidearms and a supervisor they answer to. Officers all spawn mindshielded and tracked, carry less-lethal universal pistols with rubber and overpressure magazines, and get Frontier Uplink Coins to spend at an NFSD uplink store on tiered hardsuits gated by rank. The department also has its own job: hunting smugglers by finding Syndicate dead drops and contraband drop pods scattered around the sector, scanning them with a detective's contraband forensics module to pay spesos and coins into the department's bank, and tracking DNA back to the smugglers, while senior ranks get ship vouchers to claim NFSD patrol craft.

- **Take** — It turns a flat three-role Security department into an eight-rank career ladder with a reward loop of its own (smuggling interdiction paying into a department bank), giving security players progression and something proactive to do when nobody is committing crimes.
- **Skip** — The roles are inseparable from Frontier's sector economy: uplink coins, department bank payouts, ship vouchers, dead-drop/drop-pod smuggling content and a whole bespoke NFSD wardrobe and weapon set all have to come along, and departments.yml rewires upstream department membership on top of that.
- **Touches** — security, command, medical, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [player-economy-and-banking](#player-economy-and-banking), [shipyard-player-ships](#shipyard-player-ships), [access-control-rework](#access-control-rework), [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack)
- **Verified** — supported; assessed as entangled, corrected by the verifier. I fetched all 11 cited files plus browsed the repo tree, and confirmed almost everything in the gameplay description directly (rank ladder, mindshield+tracking implants, less-lethal rubber/overpressure magazines, FrontierUplinkCoin currency and hardsuit BuyerJobCondition rank gating, ShipVoucherNfsdBailiff/Sergeant, ContrabandForensicsModule, dead-drop/drop-pod smuggling loop paying spesos+FUCs into an NFSD bank per Smuggling.xml). But the seed's drop-in premise (zero files outside the fork's own namespace) is actually false for this unit: the department-restructure's Security role list is rewritten in place inside Resources/Prototypes/Roles/Jobs/departments.yml, a non-_NF, non-namespaced upstream path, with '# Frontier' diff-marker comments replacing the vanilla Security roster. The claim's own cited departments.yml (the _NF one) doesn't contain this edit at all — it cited the wrong file for that specific assertion, but the underlying behavior (upstream rewiring) is real and lives outside the namespace, which is stronger than merely 'entangled with sibling systems.'
  - unsupported: "departments.yml rewires upstream department membership on top of that" — the cited evidence file (Resources/Prototypes/_NF/Roles/Jobs/departments.yml) only defines the fork's new "Frontier" and "Antag" departments; it contains no Security entry at all and does not do the rewiring described. The actual rewiring lives in a DIFFERENT file the claim never cited: the non-namespaced Resources/Prototypes/Roles/Jobs/departments.yml (no _NF/ prefix), which directly comments out upstream Security roles (Detective, HeadOfSecurity, SecurityCadet, SecurityOfficer, Warden, SecurityGuard) and replaces them in place with the NFSD job IDs, each tagged '# Frontier'. This is a real file living outside the fork's own namespace directories, which falsifies the seed's premise for this unit.
