### Melee weapons, shields and blocking

<a id="melee-weapons-and-blocking"></a>

Added melee weapons plus the defensive layer around them: rechargeable shields, blocking, parrying, whetstones and personal energy shields.

**goobstation**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Melee fights stop being pure damage trades and become about what you are holding. Goob adds a big rack of hand weapons (a crafted hammer, canes, sabres, the Head of Security's self-recharging "Justice" energy sword that can be flipped between a cutting mode and a stun mode, a Syndicate contractor baton that ion-shocks borgs and staggers people, flatcaps with glass shards sewn into the brim in plasma/uranium variants, boomerangs that fly back to your hand after a throw) alongside a defensive layer: the Blueshield's battery-powered greatshield soaks a large fraction of incoming blunt, slash, piercing and heat damage even when you are not actively blocking, drains its charge doing so, hums and glows while raised, and has a slot to sheath your baton in. There are also gloves that teach the wearer Krav Maga while worn. Some blades cut deeper wounds than their raw damage suggests, and several of these are theft-target or contraband items that antagonists and security both want.

- **Take** — It turns melee from a damage-number exchange into a real defensive game (passive vs active blocking, shield charge as a resource, weapons with two firing modes) and hands security and antags a much wider, more characterful weapon roster.
- **Skip** — The good parts lean on Goob's own extensions to blocking, item-switching and the wound system rather than upstream's plain BlockingComponent, so you cannot take just the shields without porting or reimplementing that combat plumbing, and rebalancing a whole new melee roster against your existing security kit is ongoing work.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, command, service, all-players
- **Needs** — [martial-arts](#martial-arts), [surgery-and-limb-wounds](#surgery-and-limb-wounds)
- **Verified** — partly supported. shields.yml, contractor_baton.yml, justice.yml, bladed_caps.yml, hammer.yml, kravmagagloves.yml and BoomerangComponent.cs all read exactly as claimed (passive/active block fractions, battery-drain-on-block, baton sheath slot, StunBorgsOnHit+Ion:35 on the contractor baton, self-recharging battery on Justice, plasma/uranium bladed flatcaps, Construction-graph hammer, ClothingGrantComponent Krav Maga, boomerang return fields). But going beyond the cited evidence to check the C# backing the shields: Content.Shared/Blocking/BlockingSystem.cs sits at the literal upstream path (no _Goobstation prefix) and, diffed against space-wizards/space-station-14 master, is substantially rewritten with an inline "// Goobstation" marker on an added ItemToggleSystem dependency and older API idioms than current upstream -- a fork copy patching an upstream-path file in place, not a self-contained addition, exactly the case path-data can't see. RechargeableBlocking additionally lives under Content.Server/_White/Blocking (a merged sub-fork, not _Goobstation), and the Justice sword's ItemSwitch plumbing lives under Content.Shared/_Shitmed/ItemSwitch (the wound/surgery sub-fork) -- neither is drop-in-clean even by the fork's own internal boundaries.
  - unsupported: "the Head of Security's self-recharging 'Justice' energy sword that can be flipped between a cutting mode and a stun mode" -- justice.yml's ItemSwitch actually defines five states (off/stun/ion/lethal/empowered), including an ion mode and an explosive 'empowered' mode with ExplodeOnMeleeHit; describing it as a two-mode cutting/stun toggle understates what the file shows.
  - unsupported: "a crafted hammer, canes, sabres" -- canes and sabres are not among the evidence_cited paths and were not found in any file fetched; only the hammer (hammer.yml) is verified.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Instead of every swing and shot landing automatically, characters carry a dodge rating: small or invisible targets are harder to hit, big ones easier, and lying down makes you easier to hit, with a separate looser roll for shots from your own side so friendly fire past a squadmate often whiffs. On the alien side, several castes get a temporary absorbing shield they pay plasma for and trigger as an ability, with its own overlay on the sprite, a shatter sound when it runs out and a slow drain that eats it if nobody hits you; some flavours also soak explosions for a couple of seconds. Melee itself gets a training layer, so a trained marine hits harder with the same weapon than an untrained one, some weapons stun on contact or do bonus damage against particular targets, some creatures shrug off punches entirely, you cannot hit yourself unless you opt in, and a dedicated key fires a held weapon's special function without digging through a right-click menu.

- **Take** — The dodge-and-shield layer turns flat trade-blows melee into something with defensive counterplay, and the separate friendly-fire miss chance is a genuinely elegant fix for the "my own team shot me in the back" problem.
- **Skip** — It is built for a Colonial Marines round, not a station one: the shields are alien caste powers keyed to a hive and plasma economy, and the melee damage scaling reads from a marine skills/rank system, so grafting it means bringing those systems or gutting the parts that make it interesting.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, command
- **Needs** — [xenomorph-hive](#xenomorph-hive), [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**sunrise**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Sunrise fills out the melee shelf with weapons that each change a fight in a specific way: a pocketable energy shield you flick open to soak hits and bounce lasers until its battery runs dry (it recharges itself after a lull), a two-handed double energy sword that only lights up when wielded and blocks well while held, a wieldable double stun baton that knocks the wind out of two people at once, a switchblade that flicks from pocket-lint to stabbing knife, a heavy breaching hammer that pries airlocks open and caves in structures when swung two-handed, a shock-cane, and an exotic scythe that flings molten projectiles while wielded. A whetstone-style sharpener puts a temporary edge on any slashing or piercing weapon for a limited number of swings, then the blade visibly dulls and the stone wears out. Some syndicate weapons are biocoded, so a crewmember who loots an energy sword off a dead traitor cannot swing it at all.

- **Take** — Rechargeable pocket shields, sharpening and biocoded antag weapons give melee fights a defensive and upkeep layer instead of pure damage trading, and most of it is item prototypes rather than new mechanics.
- **Skip** — It is a grab bag, not a coherent system: 155 files where individual pieces quietly reach into the fork's other content (the abductor silencer needs the abductor tag and mute toxin, the double energy sword doubles as a Sunrise surgery scalpel, the sharpening component lives in their blood cult namespace), and nothing reaches players until you also edit upstream uplink, vendor and loadout catalogs to stock it.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, cargo, salvage
- **Needs** — [abductors](#abductors), [surgery-and-limb-wounds](#surgery-and-limb-wounds)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**serbia-strong**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Shields stop being passive luck and become something you actively hold up: a dedicated keybind raises the shield, an "Active Blocking" indicator appears on your HUD, your character visibly changes stance, and your chance to stop incoming melee and gunfire jumps (a riot shield goes from roughly a coin-flip to blocking about three attacks in four), at the cost of not being able to throw anything while braced. Shields now have their own condition you can examine and they wear down under fire, eventually shattering into steel, glass, planks or webbing depending on what they were made of, and there is a full ladder of them: cardboard, wooden buckler, makeshift steel, web, riot, laser-dissipating, heavy ballistic (which slows you down), collapsible telescopic, mirror, clockwork, and toggleable energy shields including a syndicate red variant. Armour and shields also work by threshold rather than percentage, so a weak hit bounces off entirely while a heavy one punches through, holding a raised shield resists the instant stun from batons, and some armour converts part of a stunbaton's stamina hit into shock damage instead of dropping you outright.

- **Take** — It turns melee and close-range fights from a stat-check into an active exchange, and specifically defangs the stunbaton-into-cuffs instant-win that dominates security versus antagonist encounters.
- **Skip** — It replaces the whole armour and stun pipeline, not just shields: threshold armour, stamina conversion and instastun resistance rebalance every existing weapon, armour set and antagonist kit at once, and it needs a new keybind wired into upstream input handling, so you inherit a permanent balance-tuning job.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, cargo
- **Verified** — supported. I found direct edits to three genuinely-upstream, non-SS220 files: Content.Shared/Input/ContentKeyFunctions.cs (a vanilla file listing dozens of engine key functions) has 'ToggleActiveBlocking' spliced in at line 11 among stock entries like Drop/ExamineEntity/FocusChat; Resources/keybinds.yml has a block literally fenced '#SS220 shield rework begin' binding ToggleActiveBlocking to key B; and Content.Client/Input/ContentContexts.cs adds 'human.AddFunction(ContentKeyFunctions.ToggleActiveBlocking); //SS220 shield rework'. That settles needs-upstream-edits as fork-patches-upstream, not just integration-would-require-upstream-edits. The gameplay claim itself checks out in fine detail: shields.yml shows 11 named shield prototypes (Riot, RiotLaser, RiotBullet, WoodenBuckler, Card/cardboard, Makeshift/steel, Web, Clockwork, Mirror, Energy, Telescopic) each with AltArmor tresholdDict/durabilityTresholdDict (threshold not percentage) and Destructible behaviors spawning SheetSteel/SheetGlass, MaterialWoodPlank, or MaterialWebSilk on break; RiotBulletShieldAlt has ClothingSpeedModifier/HeldSpeedModifier 0.9 (the slow-you-down heavy ballistic shield); EnergyShieldAlt is ItemToggle-based and syndi.yml adds a red-lit EnergyShieldSyndi variant; BaseShieldAlt carries InstastunResist/InstastunResistOnActiveBlocking (baton-stun resistance while blocking); StaminaConvertArmorComponent.cs literally converts a DamageCoefficient of stamina damage into a configured DamageType (e.g. Shock); blocking.yml is the 'Active Blocking' HUD alert; and SharedAltBlockingSystem.User.cs wires the toggle to ContentKeyFunctions.ToggleActiveBlocking and cancels ThrowAttemptEvent while blocking (can't throw while braced). Only minor imprecisions found, noted in overclaims.
  - unsupported: "your chance to stop incoming melee and gunfire jumps (a riot shield goes from roughly a coin-flip to blocking about three attacks in four)" blends two different stats: RiotShieldAlt's actual AltBlocking values are rangeBlockProb 0.50 -> activeRangeBlockProb 0.60 (ranged/gunfire), and meleeBlockProb 0.65 -> activeMeleeBlockProb 0.75 (melee). No single stat goes from 0.50 to 0.75; the claim cherry-picks the passive number from one axis (ranged) and the active number from the other (melee) to build a punchier 'coin-flip to 3-in-4' line.
  - unsupported: "there is a full ladder of them" implies an exhaustive list, but the fork also ships a Dagon's Scale cult shield (Resources/Prototypes/SS220/Entities/Objects/Shields/dagots_scale.yml, parent BaseShieldAlt) that isn't in the enumerated ladder.

### Explosion rework and effects

<a id="explosion-rework"></a>

Changes how explosions propagate and how they read on screen: directional obstruction-aware blasts, shockwave overlays, explosion effect entities and shrapnel projectiles.

**stellar-station**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 9 forks carry it*

Explosions hit harder to look at and to stand near. A blast throws a red-orange flash and a rippling shockwave that visibly warps the screen, shakes your camera (harder the closer you are), and plays a different, muffled boom for people far enough away to only hear it. Small blasts no longer just chew tiles: they knock you off your feet, stun you, and fling you away from the centre, but only if you actually had line of sight to it, so ducking behind a wall or a locker genuinely protects you. Some bombs also spray shrapnel outward in all directions as real flying projectiles, and different explosive types (standard, fire, demolition, EMP, and several unique device blasts) each get their own coloured flash, light, and shockwave feel.

- **Take** — Cover suddenly matters in a firefight: obstruction-checked knockback, knockdown and stun turn grenades from a damage number into a positional threat, and the shockwave/screenshake presentation makes every explosion read instantly.
- **Skip** — It is a second explosion system living alongside upstream's, so every grenade, bomb and explosive device you want it on needs its prototype rewired, and the screenshake half pulls in a separate camera-effects system vendored from another fork.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, engineering, salvage, all-players
- **Needs** — [visual-feedback-effects](#visual-feedback-effects)
- **Verified** — partly supported. The fork's own explosion-rework files (explosions.yml, StellarExplosiveComponent.cs, SharedStellarExplosionSystem.cs) are self-contained under _ST/Content.Stellar.Shared and do not edit any upstream/vanilla prototype file in place — grenades.yml and Bombs/*.yml still carry only the plain vanilla Explosive component, unmodified. But SharedStellarExplosionSystem.cs has a real compile-time dependency on Content.Shared/_ES/Camera/ESScreenshakeSystem.cs, a separately-authored (SPDX 'Mirrorcult', MIT-licensed) subsystem outside the _ST namespace that the evidence_cited list omits entirely, confirming the claim's own depends_on: visual-feedback-effects. Giving the new system the game-wide reach the gameplay text implies would require editing a target codebase's own upstream grenade/bomb prototypes, since as-is only a couple of fork-specific ForkFiltered antagonist grenades use it.
  - unsupported: "only if you actually had line of sight to it, so ducking behind a wall or a locker genuinely protects you" — in SharedStellarExplosionSystem.cs the obstruction check (_interaction.InRangeUnobstructed) gates ONLY the physical knockback throw. Knockdown and Stun are applied in a separate loop over all entities in range with no obstruction check whatsoever (only a whitelist filter), so cover does not protect you from being knocked down or stunned, only (sometimes) from being physically flung. One shipped prototype (StellarGrenadeGorlexC4) even sets `ignoreObstruction: true`, explicitly disabling the one check that does exist.
  - unsupported: "Small blasts no longer just chew tiles: they knock you off your feet, stun you, and fling you away" reads as a description of explosions in general, but as actually wired in the repo the StellarExplosive/StellarShockwave system is applied only to a handful of new fork-specific antagonist grenades (StellarGrenadeCybersunEmp/Flash, StellarGrenadeGorlexExplosive/C4 — all tagged `categories: [ForkFiltered]`). The vanilla frag grenade, flashbang, minibomb and all the Bombs/*.yml prototypes (Resources/Prototypes/Entities/Objects/Weapons/Throwable/grenades.yml, .../Bombs/*.yml) are untouched and still use only the plain upstream `Explosive` component with no knockback/knockdown/stun/shrapnel/shockwave at all.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 9 forks carry it*

Explosions stop being a flat circle of damage and become a thing you read and react to: a nearby blast punches a visible shockwave ripple across your screen, throws shrapnel streaks outward, and burns scorch marks into the floor around it (darker at the centre, lighter at the edges, and moppable afterwards). A blast happening far away plays a distant rumble instead of the same close-up bang, so you can tell roughly how far off the boom was, and heavy ordnance like mortar and orbital-strike blasts is tuned to chew through walls rather than just hurt people. On the throwing end, priming a grenade makes your character shout a warning that everyone nearby sees and hears, and grenades gain new ways to go off: on landing after a throw, after flying a set distance, when the round is shot out of a launcher, or on a sword impact. Cluster and shrapnel grenades scatter their payload in a configurable arc (including backwards when they bounce off something). Separately you can plant a claymore mine, which takes a few seconds to place and arm, ignores your own side, blows up if someone shoots it twice, and can be carefully disarmed with a pulsing tool. Some explosives implode instead, dragging everything nearby into the centre before they go off.

- **Take** — It makes every explosion in the game legible: screen shockwave, shrapnel, floor scorching and near-vs-far blast audio give players real feedback about where a bomb went off and how close it was, and the trigger variants (throw-end, distance, shot-to-arm, claymores) turn explosives from one-shot timers into placeable, plannable tools.
- **Skip** — It is built for a Colonial Marines game, not a station one: claymores and cluster payloads key off RMC-14's own IFF faction system, the explosion presets are tuned around mortars and orbital bombardment, and the trigger layer hooks upstream TriggerEvent and extends the upstream ProjectileGrenadeComponent as a partial, so you cannot take the pretty half (shockwave, shrapnel, scorch, far-sound) without either untangling it from the marine content or accepting the whole 150-file block and its upstream-touching edges.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, cargo
- **Needs** — [firearms-content-expansion](#firearms-content-expansion)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**goobstation**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 9 forks carry it*

Blasts stop being one generic orange boom: a supermatter breach throws a green, radiation-laced shockwave, a tesla arc detonates in cyan sparks, a rigged pizza box makes a tight thump that scorches people without tearing the floor open, and a molotov you build and light by hand shatters into a spreading pool of fire that clings to anyone who runs through it. Flashbangs now knock you flat and stun you on a sliding scale by how close you were standing, so hugging the grenade is far worse than being across the room, and wearing earmuffs or a helmet with hearing protection shrinks the radius that can floor you (examining the gear tells you how much it protects). Bombs can also be wired to go off on a spoken code phrase, on being punched, or on being dropped, so an antagonist can hand someone a "gift" that only detonates when the right person says the right word.

- **Take** — Flashbang knockdown that scales with distance and is reduced by hearing protection turns "throw bang, everyone falls" into a positioning-and-gear fight, and the extra explosion types make each hazard read distinctly on screen instead of every boom looking the same.
- **Skip** — The flashbang half hooks into upstream's flash system (AreaFlashEvent, FlashSystem resistance tags) and a fork-side Common/Shared flash layer, so it is not a clean drop-in and will need re-patching every time upstream touches flashes or stuns; several explosion prototypes also assume fork content (supermatter, thermobaric launcher, molotov construction graph and RSI) that would arrive as dead entries otherwise.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, engineering, service
- **Verified** — partly supported. Fetched Goob-Station's own Content.Shared/Flash/SharedFlashSystem.cs (an upstream-namespaced path, not under _Goobstation/) and found it directly edited in place with \"// Goob edit\" / \"// Goobstation\" markers adding `AreaFlashEvent` and the `IgnoreResistancesTag` ProtoId that Content.Goobstation.Server/Flashbang/FlashbangSystem.cs subscribes to and checks. Diffing against vanilla space-wizards/space-station-14's Content.Shared/Flash/SharedFlashSystem.cs confirms these symbols do not exist upstream — they were added by Goob-Station directly inside the upstream file/namespace, which the path-data evidence (limited to _Goobstation-prefixed paths) could not see. This confirms the claim's own skip_because reasoning and downgrades the seed's \"drop-in\" guess to needs-upstream-edits, specifically the fork-patches-upstream variant.
  - unsupported: "Bombs can also be wired to go off on a spoken code phrase, on being punched, or on being dropped" — the "on being dropped" trigger is not backed by the cited evidence or by anything found in Content.Goobstation.Server/Explosion/Components/: the only similarly-named component, OnTrigger/DropOnTriggerComponent.cs, does the opposite (it drops the entity's held items when the bomb triggers, it is not a trigger fired by the bomb itself being dropped). "Spoken code phrase" (TriggerOnVoiceIdLockedComponent) and "on being punched" (TriggerOnMeleeComponent.cs, confirmed to exist) both check out; the drop-trigger claim does not.

### Grabs, shoves and close-quarters mechanics

<a id="close-quarters-combat-rework"></a>

Reworks hands-on combat with escalating grabs, shove impacts, tackles, knockdowns, backstabs, point-blank executions and a contest system that resolves who wins.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 11 forks carry it*

Shoving stops being a one-shot coin flip and becomes a wrestling match: you barge into someone repeatedly, each attempt announcing itself to everyone nearby ("you try to tackle X", "X tackles you down"), and after a few failed shoves the next one is guaranteed to slam them flat and paralyse them for a couple of seconds. Anyone knocked down, stunned, unconscious or freshly tackled can be rescued by a buddy grabbing them with an empty hand and shaking them, which shaves seconds off the timer and pops them back upright, though someone whose stamina is gone just flops straight back down. Shoving an armed person while untrained in close-quarters combat can make their gun go off by accident, and being dazed drops a dark vignette over your screen that squeezes how far you can see, while wielding a gun paints a crosshair on your cursor and targeting lasers visibly mark whoever they are painted on.

- **Take** — It turns shoving from a random stun into a legible back-and-forth with escalating odds and a teammate-driven counterplay (shaking someone awake), so hands-on fights become a group activity rather than a coin flip.
- **Skip** — The tackle path is gated on RMC's own skills, stamina, standing/rest and xeno-infection systems and fires off a fork-replaced disarm event, so grafting it means either dragging those systems along or rewriting the contest rules against upstream's disarm and stamina model.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, antagonist, medical
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [xenomorph-hive](#xenomorph-hive), [mob-damage-and-movement](#mob-damage-and-movement)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**starlight**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 11 forks carry it*

Standing over someone who is cuffed or already down, a right-click option appears to finish them off with a sharp weapon or a loaded gun: a short wind-up prints a message everyone nearby can read, and if nobody interrupts it, the victim dies. Aimed at yourself, the same option becomes a suicide. Heavy guns now shove the person firing them, throwing you backwards a tile or two and draining stamina with each shot unless you are wearing magboots, and examining the weapon tells you in advance how far it will push you and how much stamina that costs. Melee weapons can carry payloads that inject chemicals into whoever they connect with or clamp cuffs onto a target after a brief struggle, and shots that fail to punch through armour throw off sparks and a ricochet clang instead of wounding you. Despite the feature name, this bundle is not a grab or shove rework, there is no escalating grab ladder here.

- **Take** — Executions and shooter recoil both make fights feel decisive rather than attritional, and the on-hit payload components are a cheap generic hook any fork can hang new weapons off (chem syringe batons, capture guns) without writing new systems.
- **Skip** — It is a grab-bag rather than the coherent close-quarters rework the name promises, and several pieces (armour sparks, pierce levels, friction status, stun baton visuals) are written against Starlight's own ballistics and stun rework, so grafting them means either dragging that in too or rewriting them against upstream hitscan and SharedStunSystem.
- **Touches** — security, antagonist, all-players, medical
- **Needs** — [firearms-content-expansion](#firearms-content-expansion)
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as needs upstream edits, corrected by the verifier. Beyond what the claim states, I found the fork does not merely need upstream edits to integrate — its own tree already patches at least three vanilla-path files in place: Content.Shared/Execution/ExecutionComponent.cs (marked \"//STARLIGHT MODIFIED FILE ... TODO: Use upstream and modify it accordingly instead\", converting instance fields to statics and splitting Melee/Gun variants that vanilla's current ExecutionComponent.cs does not have), Content.Shared/Weapons/Ranged/Systems/SharedGunSystem.cs (adds the OnNonEmptyGunShotEvent the knockback system depends on — not even in the cited evidence list), and Content.Shared/Stunnable/SharedStunSystem.cs (extensive \"// Starlight edit start/end\" blocks adding a voluntary-knockdown parameter, referenced via FrictionStatusComponent's Access(typeof(SharedStunSystem)) restriction). Combined with ArmorSparkEffectComponent's own doc comment tying it to Starlight's own SP/HP ammo pierce levels, this is a bundle threaded through the fork's own ballistics and stun rework plus three separately-modified vanilla files, not a clean self-contained needs-upstream-edits unit.
  - unsupported: "unless you are wearing magboots" — the cited SharedKnockbackSystem.cs only checks for a generic NoSlipComponent (worn item or self) via CheckForNoSlips(); no cited file or YAML ties that component specifically to magboots, and none of the cited files name which item grants it.
  - unsupported: "Heavy guns now shove the person firing them" — the mechanic is driven by KnockbackByUserTagComponent matching arbitrary TagPrototype keys in a dictionary; no cited evidence shows this is restricted to a 'heavy guns' category as opposed to any tagged weapon.

## Engineering

### Research tree and lathe rework

<a id="research-tree-rework"></a>

Replaces the technology console with a different progression model, whether a searchable node graph, new disciplines, per-department fabricators or transferable research disks.

**rmc14**

*structural · entangled · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Instead of researching tech in a lab, the crew earns progress by going out and recovering things: intel documents, uploaded data, retrieved items, analyzed chemical samples, rescued survivors, recovered bodies, and restoring the colony's power and comms. All of that feeds a shared points pool that command spends at a Tech Control console, picking from tiered unlock nodes with icons, prices that climb each time a repeatable node is bought, and some nodes time-locked until later in the round. Buying a node has an immediate round-wide effect rather than adding a lathe recipe: more supply budget, more dropship fabricator budget, an emergency supply drop, a vehicle unlocked, or a fresh squad of reinforcement marines spawning as ghost roles from cryo, each purchase announced station-wide and logged under the buyer's name. Separately, a handful of research data disks can be found and slotted into medical machines to teach an autodoc new procedures (internal bleeding, broken bones, organ damage, parasite extraction) or upgrade a sleeper's chemical range and dialysis.

- **Take** — It converts "research" from a lab minigame into a round-wide objective economy, where everyone's fieldwork buys reinforcements, budget and vehicles that visibly change how the rest of the round goes.
- **Skip** — It is not a tech tree you can lift in isolation: the console is wired into RMC-14's intel objectives, requisitions budget, dropship fabricator, ARES logging, marine announcements, player-count scaling and cryo reinforcement spawners, so grafting it means either importing or re-authoring all of those, and the actual node catalogue is not in prototypes, so every option would have to be rebuilt for your own fork's content.
- **Touches** — command, cargo, medical, ghost-roles, all-players
- **Needs** — [cargo-bounties-and-market](#cargo-bounties-and-market), [gamemaster-and-ghost-roles](#gamemaster-and-ghost-roles), [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [autodoc](#autodoc)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**backmen**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Science works off a rebuilt research console: a pannable node map of technologies you can recenter, filter to already-researched entries, and click to see a card showing the exact recipes it unlocks and the cost in research points. Instead of just needing enough points, a technology can be gated behind "research 3 more Tier 2 Engineering technologies to reach 75%", and once the station commits to a main discipline the higher tiers of the other disciplines grey out with a message saying that branch is locked. The tree itself is a fork-written progression spread across disciplines for engineering, security/arsenal, cargo, mining, medical, robotics and science, so what each department can eventually build, and in what order, is different from the usual station. Unlocking a technology also gets announced with the name and the points spent, and the package ships alongside a talking Oracle statue that demands specific items ("I demand one syringe! Do not question why!") and pays out in research knowledge when fed.

- **Take** — It turns research from a points-shopping list into a routed choice with real consequences, since committing to a main discipline visibly locks out the deep end of the others and forces science to pick a direction for the round.
- **Skip** — It is not a bolt-on: the fork replaces the whole technology tree and the console UI, so every upstream technology and lathe recipe would need re-slotting into their layout and hand-placed node positions, and every future upstream research change becomes a merge conflict you own forever.
- **Touches** — science, engineering, cargo, security, medical, salvage
- **Needs** — [psionics-and-glimmer](#psionics-and-glimmer)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**goobstation**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

The research console stops being a flat list and becomes a map you drag around and zoom in and out of, with every technology sitting as a card connected to its prerequisites; locked tech you have not qualified for is hidden behind TV static, and a "Recenter" button snaps you back if you get lost. Clicking a card shows its point cost, its prerequisites, and picture cards of every recipe it will unlock, plus a percentage showing how far through that tier the station is, and a toggle to show only what has already been researched. The tree itself is much bigger than upstream's: scientists can chase mech chassis lines (Ripley MK-II, Clarke, and Gygax/Durand which need CentComm to sign off), MOD suits, energy-cell pistols/SMGs/snipers/LMGs, bananium weapons, plumbing, cloning, xenobiology gear and bluespace kit, and lathes announce "database has been updated with N new recipes" as each unlock lands, with recipes sorted into new fabricator categories per mech and suit line.

- **Take** — The pannable prerequisite-graph console with static-fogged locked nodes and recipe preview cards is a large, immediately legible upgrade over upstream's flat technology list, and it makes a long research tree readable instead of overwhelming.
- **Skip** — The console UI is only half of it: most of the technologies and lathe packs unlock Goob-specific content (mech chassis lines, MOD suits, biofab synthetic organs, energy-cell gun families), so taking the tree wholesale drags in several other fork systems, and taking only the UI means re-authoring the node graph against your own content and re-checking node positions every time you add tech.
- **Touches** — science, engineering, medical, cargo, security, command
- **Needs** — [mech-overhaul](#mech-overhaul), [modular-hardsuits](#modular-hardsuits)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**far-horizons**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Science works from a visual, searchable tech map instead of the stock discipline console: nodes sit in three tiers, each with an icon, a point cost and prerequisite lines to earlier nodes, and you pick one and click Research to see a progress bar fill toward its cost. Points collect in a station bank that has a hard ceiling and bleeds away once full, so the console nags over the radio that the bank is maxed and someone needs to queue work; scientists line up to three technologies in a queue (more once queue-size upgrades are bought), and every queue add, removal and completed unlock is broadcast on the radio with the approver's name. Alongside the usual lathe recipes, some nodes hand out station-wide perks rather than items, such as unlocking plastic surgery, making surgery faster, or raising the point bank and queue limits, and higher tiers only open after the station has banked enough total research.

- **Take** — It turns research from a menu of purchases into a visible map with pacing pressure, a bank that punishes idling and radio chatter that makes science's progress legible to the whole station.
- **Skip** — It replaces the research console wholesale, so every lathe recipe's tech gating has to be re-mapped onto the new node graph, and the shipped tree assumes fork content (shuttle guns, mechs, cyberlimbs, bananium weapons) plus a vendored Starlight discipline set you would have to prune or reproduce.
- **Touches** — science, engineering, medical, security, cargo, command
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**monolith**

*loop-change · entangled · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Research stops being one science tree and becomes a set of rival manufacturer catalogues: you first buy a shared spine of tiered "Material Research" and "Ballistics Research" nodes, then branch into a corporation or faction you want to arm yourself from, each with its own T1-T3 ladder priced in the tens of thousands of points. Mieyo sells breaching gear, wound treatment and bionics; the pirate PDV branch sells ballistics, thermal imaging, a portable hacking tool and blood-red imitation suits; other branches sell energy weapons, hardsuits, mechs, drones, and warship munitions like torpedoes, railgun shells and fighter ordnance. Several nodes unlock shipyard vouchers by grade rather than an item, so researching far enough is how a crew earns the right to buy bigger ships. A second console turns stored research points back into physical 1k, 5k and 10k data disks after a short print cycle, so points become objects you can pocket, hand to another crew or feed into building "data farm" machines instead of only spending them at your own lathe.

- **Take** — The point-disk printer is the genuinely portable idea here: making research points into a physical, printable, tradeable disk turns science output into something crews can carry, trade and steal instead of a number locked to one server.
- **Skip** — The tree itself is written for Monolith's multi-ship sector setting, with nodes whose payload is faction-branded gear, ship vouchers and torpedo ammunition that do not exist on a station fork, so grafting the disciplines means importing or reinventing hundreds of entity prototypes before a single node unlocks anything.
- **Touches** — science, engineering, cargo, security, medical, command, all-players
- **Needs** — [shipyard-player-ships](#shipyard-player-ships), [ship-to-ship-combat](#ship-to-ship-combat)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Construction and crafting

<a id="construction-and-crafting"></a>

Multi-step construction graphs for walls, machines and furniture plus hand-crafting recipes and rapid construction devices.

**corvax, cosmatic-drift, ephemeral-space, funky, gaby-station-now-dumont-station, goobstation, impstation, monolith, rmc14, serbia-strong**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 12 forks carry it*

Across these ten forks this is mostly a large pile of extra things you can build and print: park benches, pews and sofas you place tile by tile, boarded-up windows, spider-web nests, secure trash bins, wall-mounted computers, crates and cardboard boxes, food you assemble by hand (steaks, candy), and lathe menus stocked with fork-specific clothing, ammo, tiles and even printed paperwork forms sorted into categories like "Complaints" and "Orders". A few forks go past recipes into new engineering behaviour: on one, walls and machines no longer just shatter but turn into a "broken" version that examines as repairable with a named tool, so you patch damage instead of rebuilding from sheets; on another, a charged repair tool rebuilds destroyed pieces of a ship back onto ghostly outlines of where they used to be; the marine fork gives its frontline technician a dedicated build menu for throwing up sandbag lines, barbed wire and barricades under fire and then upgrading them in place. Smaller touches include inflatable emergency walls and doors printed from durathread, flatpacks you drop and unfold into a working machine, and upgrade kits that bump an installed machine to a better tier without deconstructing it.

- **Take** — The recipe and lathe portion is pure content: it gives engineers, cargo and service crew more to build during quiet stretches without touching how any existing job is played.
- **Skip** — It is not one feature but ten unrelated fork content dumps, and the genuinely interesting parts (breakable-instead-of-destroyed walls, ship repair ghosts, field fortification building) each drag in their own systems and their own art, so you end up cherry-picking anyway and maintaining recipes that reference entities you may not have.
- **Touches** — engineering, cargo, service, security, science, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported. Re-fetched all 11 cited evidence files and they back the core mechanics well: ES's breakable.ftl literally reads "It's broken. It can be repaired using a {$tool}", Monolith's ship-repair/tool.ftl names a "repair ghost" entity and charge-based failure strings matching the claimed ghost-outline repair tool, RMC's Combat Technician guidebook page describes sandbags, barbed-wire lacing, and in-place metal/plasteel/MB-6 barricade upgrades in exactly the detail claimed, CD's furniture.yml lists BenchPark*, BenchPew*, RattySofa*/CorpSofa* construction recipes (park benches, pews, sofas confirmed), Funky's inflatables.yml prints InflatableWall/InflatableDoor from Durathread, and corvax's lathe-categories.ftl contains literal "Complaints" and "Orders" categories. That part of the claim is solid. But several illustrative specifics in the prose — boarded-up windows, spider-web nests, secure trash bins, wall-mounted computers, crates/cardboard boxes, hand-assembled steak/candy food, flatpacks, and machine upgrade kits — appear nowhere in the 11 cited files, so I could not verify them; they may exist elsewhere in these large forks but the claim states them as if directly evidenced. On graft_shape: I traced Funky's InflatableWall recipe into Resources/Prototypes/Entities/Structures/Machines/lathe.yml — the vanilla (non-namespaced) file defining the base Autolathe — and found the fork has directly inserted "- InflatableWall #funky inflatables" and several other "#funk"-tagged lines into that shared file's staticRecipes list, alongside pre-existing "Delta-V additions" markers from other forks. This proves the fork's own tree edits an upstream-path file in place (fork-patches-upstream), and separately, wiring any of these lathe recipes into another codebase would require editing that codebase's own lathe/autolathe recipe list too (integration-would-require-upstream-edits) — both readings hold. This directly refutes the "drop-in" seed and confirms the assessor's "needs-upstream-edits" call.
  - unsupported: boarded-up windows
  - unsupported: spider-web nests
  - unsupported: secure trash bins
  - unsupported: wall-mounted computers
  - unsupported: crates and cardboard boxes
  - unsupported: food you assemble by hand (steaks, candy)
  - unsupported: flatpacks you drop and unfold into a working machine
  - unsupported: upgrade kits that bump an installed machine to a better tier without deconstructing it

### Power grid and device networking

<a id="power-grid-and-device-networking"></a>

Cable-based power distribution with APCs, SMES units, substations and generators, plus wired and wireless device networks with link configurators. A console is only here if it reads or controls power or the device network; consoles that merely happen to be machines are `device-and-machine-additions`.

**rmc14**

*structural · entangled · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Power here is not a web of wires you trace with a multitool, it is a handful of big fusion reactors feeding rooms directly. Engineers keep the reactors fed with fusion fuel cells (a slow ten-second swap), and patch broken ones back together through a wrench-then-wire-then-weld repair sequence while the reactor's face shows how much juice is left. Every room has its own APC with a battery, a main breaker, a charge toggle and three separate switches for equipment, lighting and environment, each set to auto, forced on, or forced off; it is ID-locked and screwdriver-locked, and a marine without real engineering training cannot do anything with it beyond stare at the screen. The sabotage side is the drama: someone can rig a reactor to overload, at which point it starts humming and hissing warnings at anyone nearby, and an engineer with a multitool has to get there and shut it back down before the lights go out. Bulbs and tubes are physical objects that shatter when something hits them, burn your bare hand if you grab them, and simply die when the reactors do, so losing power means fighting in the dark.

- **Take** — It turns power from background plumbing into a defendable objective: a small number of reactors that can be fed, broken, repaired and sabotaged, with room-by-room APC control and lights that actually go out when the reactors do.
- **Skip** — It replaces the upstream cable-and-node power grid wholesale rather than sitting beside it, so every powered machine, every map area and every APC on your stations has to be converted; on top of that the APC and reactor interactions are hard-gated behind RMC's engineer skill levels and its own access-level ids, and the overload path assumes a xeno-style antagonist exists to trigger it.
- **Touches** — engineering, antagonist, command, all-players
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [access-control-rework](#access-control-rework)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**ephemeral-space**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Engineering's main power source is an antimatter engine: instead of a machine you tune, the antimatter is a pool sitting in a containment area that literally grows when it is fed plasma gas, creeps onto neighbouring tiles once it gets too big, eats anything that touches it with a nasty radiation-pulse crackle, and detonates if it is allowed to grow past its limit. Converter machines placed around it siphon a little of that mass off every few seconds and turn it into electricity for the grid, and a converter that takes damage stops working until someone welds it back together (examining it says the accumulator is broken). The fork also writes a proper set of in-game power guides that teach players to read a Power Monitoring Computer, multitool a cable to see supply versus demand on that specific voltage network, understand why generators ramp up slowly and cause brownouts, and set up PACMAN portable generators, plus a few small device-linking additions (keypads that fire "unlock" and "lock" signals to whatever you wire them to, and shared radio frequencies for armory, radstorm and warp-drive countdown timers).

- **Take** — It turns setting up the station engine into a live containment problem (feed it, fence it in, drain it) rather than a start-it-and-forget-it machine, and the power guidebook pages are genuinely useful teaching material regardless of which engine you run.
- **Skip** — The antimatter guidebook page is an unwritten "TODO write the rest lol" stub, so you would be porting an engine with no player-facing documentation and would have to author the containment room, converter placement and station mapping yourself before anyone can use it.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, all-players
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**starlight**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Engineering gets a bluespace harvester: a machine you dial up through ten levels, each one eating exponentially more power (1 kW at level 1, 100 MW at level 10) and paying out points you spend on randomised loot crates. Push it past level 7 and it starts tearing open portals beside itself that dump two to five hostile creatures onto the deck and lock the machine out until the mess is cleared, so the profitable settings are the ones that bite back. Separately, docking a shuttle to the station now welds the two high-voltage cable grids together automatically, so a ship can feed off station power or power the station while docked, and atmos devices (filters, mixers, pressure and volume pumps, injectors) can be wired to buttons and sensors so a tech can flip or retune a whole gas setup remotely instead of clicking each machine. Smaller additions round it out: lockers that need power and can be hacked open or electrified through a wire panel, signal ports for scan gates, mass drivers, ticket machines, body scanners and turret banks, and batteries that trickle-charge themselves.

- **Take** — The bluespace harvester is a genuine risk/reward power sink that gives engineering a reason to overbuild the grid, and cable-over-dock quietly fixes the long-standing awkwardness of powering a docked shuttle.
- **Skip** — It is three unrelated features sharing a folder — the harvester needs its own hostile mobs, portal entity and loot-pool prototypes to come with it, the atmos signal work has to be attached to upstream gas-device prototypes device by device, and the cable-docking system reaches into upstream node-group internals plus three fork CCVars, so none of it lands as a clean copy.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, cargo, security, service, medical, shuttle-crew
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Shuttle piloting and navigation extensions

<a id="shuttle-piloting-extensions"></a>

Adds autopilot, boost modes, scuttling, radar blips, docking rules and scheduled transit routes on top of manual shuttle flight.

**corvax, frontier, monolith, rmc14, serbia-strong, starlight**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 8 forks carry it*

A pilot sitting at a shuttle console gets a lot more than thrust and rotation. They can flip the engines between Cruise (drift freely), Drive (dampened, stops when you let go) and Park (locked in place), drop a marked waypoint on the radar and steer to it, search nearby contacts by IFF name, undock every airlock at once with one button, and on some forks hand the helm to an autopilot that flies to the chosen destination and pops "Autopilot Arrived" when it gets there. Ships can advertise what they are offering to everyone else's radar with little icons (music note for services aboard, dollar for a shop, smiley for a hangout), docking ports get readable names like "1A", "CB2" or "McDrive Thru" so traffic control can talk people in, and a stealth console lets a ship drop off IFF entirely for a timed window before going on cooldown. Crew without a ship of their own can read a bus timetable that tells them which numbered route is running, which bus is coming and how long until it reaches each station, then ride an NPC-scheduled shuttle between destinations. Ships also gain a self-destruct: someone arms an antimatter scuttle charge, a station-wide announcement names the ship and counts down the seconds, and anyone who can reach the device in time can spend a long fiddly do-after disarming it. RMC-14's slice is a different game entirely: crewed dropships that fly troops to a landing zone and run close air support, with fabricated cannons, guided missiles and napalm rockets, engine modules that cut refuel and travel time, and medevac and fulton mounts for pulling casualties off the ground.

- **Take** — The console-side pieces (cruise/drive/park modes, radar waypoints, IFF search, undock-all, named dock labels) are small quality-of-life wins that make flying a shuttle legible instead of a wrestling match, and they are useful on any map that has more than one destination.
- **Skip** — Every fork's version hooks into the upstream shuttle console UI and ShuttleConsoleSystem as partials/overrides, so this rides directly on the part of upstream that churns most; the genuinely exciting bits (public transit routes, dropship CAS) only pay off if you already have a multi-destination sector or a whole marine gamemode to hang them on.
- **Touches** — engineering, command, cargo, service, all-players
- **Needs** — [ruins-poi-and-sector-generation](#ruins-poi-and-sector-generation)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Supermatter engine

<a id="supermatter-engine"></a>

A crystal power source that converts gas and matter into enormous power and delaminates catastrophically when mismanaged, with its own monitoring console and warning audio.

**serbia-strong**

*structural · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Engineering gets a giant crystal as an alternative station power source, and babysitting it is a full-time job. The crystal ships inert; hitting it with an object or a shot wakes it up, after which it eats the gas around it, throws lightning arcs into tesla coils and radiation into collectors, and belches out superheated oxygen and plasma that has to be cooled and filtered by a chamber the engineers build themselves (no map ships a ready-made one). Engineers feed it matter and energy with dedicated supermatter emitters they tune by hand, and watch its integrity, internal energy, matter, temperature and per-gas effects on a monitoring console or on a PDA app anyone can install. Standing near it without meson goggles and rad protection gives you hallucinations and radiation sickness. Let its energy drift outside the safe band and integrity bleeds away: engineering radio warns first, then the whole station is told, then Central Command hears the delamination countdown, and if nobody drags it back in time the crystal explodes, or turns into a singularity or a tesla ball that starts eating the station.

- **Take** — It is the single richest engineering minigame in SS13 that upstream SS14 lacks: a permanent, multi-person job for the engineering department whose failure state is a station-wide catastrophe the whole crew has to react to.
- **Skip** — It is a ~100-file simulation (thermodynamics, per-gas response curves, integrity, delamination variants, its own emitter, console, cartridge, hardsuit and audio) that only pays off if you also commit to map work, containment-chamber design and balance tuning; a badly ported version either never delaminates or delaminates every round.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, command, all-players
- **Verified** — supported. Directly cloned the fork's tree (git ls-tree) and confirmed all ~121 SuperMatter/SuperMatterEmitter/Observer/Cartridge .cs and .yml files live under SS220/ namespace dirs, matching the drop-in path-data signal. But content-grepping non-namespace files (something path data cannot do) found the fork editing three genuinely upstream files in place: Resources/Prototypes/Guidebook/engineering.yml (adds \"- Supermatter # SS220-superMatter-crystal\" to the vanilla guide-category list), Resources/Prototypes/Research/industrial.yml, and Resources/Prototypes/Recipes/Lathes/Packs/engineering.yml (both add \"SuperMatterObserverCircuitboard # SS220 superMatter-crystal\" to vanilla tech-unlock/lathe-recipe lists). These are real edits to upstream files carrying SS220 comment tags, not new files, so this is fork-patches-upstream, not merely integration-would-require-upstream-edits. The gameplay description itself checks out almost line-for-line against the Russian guidebook text and the crystal.yml/UI-state code (inert-until-hit activation, gas consumption, LightningArcShooter/RadiationSource output to tesla coils/collectors, hot O2+plasma byproduct with \"NT currently has no standard containment chamber\" text matching \"no map ships a ready-made one,\" SuperMatterObserverUpdateState exposing integrity/energy/matter/temperature/per-gas ratios, freely-installable Cartridge-type PDA app, and the three-stage engineering-radio to station-wide to CentCom delamination warning sequence verbatim in the guidebook, ending in Explosive/Singularity/TeslaEnergyBall outcomes).
  - unsupported: "Standing near it without meson goggles and rad protection gives you hallucinations and radiation sickness" — the guidebook and crystal.yml only show a generic `EyeProtection` component check (satisfied by any eye-protection item, e.g. "engineering goggles") plus a bare `RadiationSource`; no file names a specific "meson goggles" or "rad protection" item, so the general mechanic is right but the specific gear names are the claim author's gloss, not something the evidence states.

**einstein-engines**

*structural · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Engineering gets a glowing crystal that eats the gas around it and turns it into station power, and it talks back: it announces its own integrity over the radio, growls and hums louder as it heats up, and screams "Crystal hyperstructure integrity faltering!" when engineers let the gas mix go wrong. A monitoring console shows live integrity, internal energy in eV, radiation output, absorbed moles, temperature versus its limit, waste multiplier and per-gas breakdown, with a status readout climbing from Normal through Caution, Warning, Danger, Emergency to Delaminating. Anyone who walks into it is flashed into a pile of ash, a saboteur can carve a sliver off the crystal (which halves the delamination timer and triggers a Central Command announcement blaming Engineering), and if nobody saves it the countdown runs out and it delaminates into a station-wrecking explosion, a singularity, a tesla ball, or an unstoppable resonance cascade depending on how it died.

- **Take** — It is the one engine that makes power generation an ongoing, tense job rather than a setup task: it degrades on its own, it announces its own decline to the whole station, and a bad shift ends the round, so Engineering has a reason to stay at their post all round.
- **Skip** — It is a large, tightly-coupled simulation (98 files, its own console UI, hundreds of audio clips, sliver/kudzu/lightning/singularity/tesla spawn chains) whose difficulty curve is tuned against Einstein Engines' atmos and map layouts, so grafting it means owning a balance surface you will be retuning for months, and its prototype also carries EE's LanguageSpeaker/LanguageKnowledge components that must be stripped or ported.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, science, antagonist, command, all-players
- **Verified** — supported. All six cited files check out and back the gameplay text closely: SupermatterComponent.cs has the gas dictionary, sliver/delam timer halving, DelamType enum (Explosion/Singulo/Tesla/Cascade), damage thresholds, radio Channel field; SupermatterStatusType.cs has the exact Normal->Caution->Warning->Danger->Emergency->Delaminating ladder; supermatter-console.ftl has every stat the claim lists (integrity, Internal Energy in eV, Radiation Emission, Absorbed Moles, Temperature vs Temperature Limit, Waste Multiplier, per-gas Absorption); supermatter.ftl has the quoted \"Crystal hyperstructure integrity faltering!\" line and the CC tamper announcement. I additionally fetched supermatter.yml (the entity prototype, not separately cited) and found it declares `LanguageSpeaker`/`LanguageKnowledge` components, exactly as skip_because claims. Crucially, those two component types are NOT defined under `_EE/`, they live at Content.Shared/Language/Components/LanguageSpeakerComponent.cs (verified 200 OK) which is EE's own separately-namespaced language system, outside the six cited evidence paths and outside any `_EE` prefix the path-data heuristic would have scanned. That is a real dependency the path data couldn't see, which is exactly why the seed's drop-in guess needs downgrading and why the claim's own "needs-upstream-edits" label is the right call.
  - unsupported: skip_because states "hundreds of audio clips" — the cited SoundCollections/supermatter.yml file contains only ~66 .ogg file references across its two accent collections (normal + delam), plus a handful of standalone loop/sting sounds referenced in the component. That's dozens, not hundreds.

**backmen**

*structural · entangled · high confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Engineering gets a glowing crystal reactor instead of just flipping on a generator: emitters shoot it, radiation collectors soak up the output as station power, and the crystal's health depends entirely on the gas mix engineers keep pumped around it. Nitrogen and coolants keep it calm, plasma or tritium cook it, and it eats anything that touches it, so the job becomes running the freezer/filter/scrubber loop and watching a dedicated Supermatter Monitoring Console that shows integrity, internal energy, radiation, absorbed moles, temperature and a per-gas breakdown while the status ticks from Normal through Caution, Warning, Danger, Emergency. If it gets away from you, the crystal screams station-wide countdown warnings ("X Seconds Remain Before Delamination") and then delaminates as a huge explosion, a singularity, a tesla or a full cascade, wiping a large chunk of the station. Side content includes buildable angled reflectors that bounce emitter bolts, and clothing that stops you being disintegrated on contact.

- **Take** — It turns engineering from a start-the-generator chore into a live, failable job with a real station-ending failure state, and it ships the whole package: crystal, console UI, alarm audio, reflectors, flatpack construction and a guidebook.
- **Skip** — As written it will not compile without the fork's exotic-gas expansion (the gas tables reference BZ, Pluoxium, Hydrogen, Nitrium, Healium, HyperNoblium, ProtoNitrate, Zauker, Halon, Helium and AntiNoblium, none of which exist upstream), and beyond that it needs map work (a containment chamber, emitters, collectors, cooling loop) plus a translation pass since the guidebook is Russian-only; the tuning is also a long balance tail, since a badly tuned supermatter either never delaminates or kills the round early.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, command, all-players
- **Needs** — [exotic-gas-expansion](#exotic-gas-expansion), [atmospherics-simulation](#atmospherics-simulation)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.
