## Atmos

### Exotic gases and reactions

<a id="exotic-gas-expansion"></a>

Ports the wider gas roster (BZ, halon, healium, hyper-noblium, nitrium, pluoxium, proto-nitrate, zauker, metallic hydrogen) with their production and decomposition reactions, alarm thresholds and derived crafting materials.

**funky**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmospherics goes from a handful of gases to twenty: alongside oxygen, plasma and tritium the station can now make and handle BZ, healium, nitrium, pluoxium, hydrogen, halon, helium, proto-nitrate, zauker, hyper-noblium and anti-noblium, each with its own coloured cloud when it leaks, its own air-alarm danger rating, its own effect on anyone who breathes it, and its own cargo price per mole. A pile of new machinery gives those gases somewhere to come from and somewhere to go: a multi-part hypertorus fusion reactor driven from its own console, a crystallizer that freezes a held gas mix into solid items (metallic hydrogen, healium and proto-nitrate crystals, ammonia crystals, and at the lethal end a supermatter sliver), a fuel-fed electrolyzer, portable pipe scrubbers and gas extractors, and a temperature gate that only opens when the gas passing through is above or below a number you type in. A bluespace sender lets atmos push one custom mix into a station-wide network, and wall-mounted bluespace vendors elsewhere fill crew gas tanks straight from it.

- **Take** — It turns atmospherics from a life-support chore into a production department: twenty gases with distinct effects, real recipes that yield sellable solid products, and machinery that gives an atmos tech a whole round's worth of work.
- **Skip** — The gas roster is not additive content - it widens the engine's core gas array from 9 to 20 slots and rewrites the shared atmos constants file, so every future upstream atmos merge conflicts in the hottest, most performance-sensitive code in the game, and the simulation cost of the wider array is paid every tick on every tile.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, cargo, medical, science
- **Needs** — [atmospherics-simulation](#atmospherics-simulation), [fusion-reactor](#fusion-reactor)
- **Verified** — partly supported. The fork's own copy patches upstream files in place: Content.Shared/Atmos/Atmospherics.cs sets TotalNumberOfGases = 20 with all 11 new gases in the Gas enum, and Resources/Prototypes/Atmospherics/gases.yml appends gases 9-19 with #funkystation pricePerMole/dangerLevel fields, so needs-upstream-edits is correct and the drop-in seed is wrong. Machinery claims (multi-part HFR with console, crystallizer recipes including a dangerous supermatter sliver, fuel-fed electrolyzer, pipe scrubber, gas extractor, typed-threshold temperature gate, bluespace sender plus wall-mounted vendors) all verified in the cited files; only the per-gas leak-cloud and breathing-effect universals overreach, and reaction code additionally lives in the _Sunrise namespace, not _Funkystation.
  - unsupported: each with its own coloured cloud when it leaks - BZ, Pluoxium, Hydrogen and Helium have no gasOverlaySprite/gasOverlayState in Resources/Prototypes/Atmospherics/gases.yml, so those gases produce no visible leak cloud; the color field only tints UI/pipe visuals
  - unsupported: its own effect on anyone who breathes it - in Resources/Prototypes/Reagents/gases.yml the Hyper-Noblium, Proto-Nitrate, Helium and Anti-Noblium reagents' only Gas-metabolism effect is an Oxygenate conditioned on OrganType Thaven, so for ordinary crew breathing them has no effect

**forky**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmospherics gets a much larger chemistry set: alongside the usual air, the station can now brew BZ, healium, nitrium, pluoxium, hyper-noblium, proto-nitrate, zauker, halon, helium and hydrogen by mixing existing gases at the right temperatures and pressures, and each one does something distinct when someone breathes it. Healium heals burns, brute and toxins and eventually puts the patient to sleep, so a flooded room can be a field hospital; BZ makes people see rainbows, cough and stumble around at two-thirds speed; nitrium is a stimulant that gets stronger the more you inhale; halon smothers fires by eating the oxygen and chilling the room; zauker is straight poison. Air alarms learn to warn about the new gases, an electrolyzer machine burns plasma or uranium sheets to split water vapour into hydrogen and oxygen (and convert hyper-noblium or BZ into other exotics), and freezing hydrogen with BZ compresses it into metal hydrogen sheets that engineers craft into a lightweight prying fire axe and a set of armour and helmet.

- **Take** — It turns atmospherics from a life-support chore into a production department with real outputs other roles want: healing gas for medical, firefighting gas for engineering, stimulants, and a craftable metal hydrogen tool and armour set at the end of the chain.
- **Skip** — Ten new gases mean every upstream gas reaction prototype, air alarm default, atmos UI readout and the shared gas enum have to grow with them, and the reagent effects lean on fork-specific reagents and status effects, so this is a permanent merge-conflict surface on files upstream keeps touching.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, medical, science, salvage, all-players
- **Verified** — supported. Fetched the cited YAML/locale/C# files and every specific claim checked out: gases.yml defines BZ (rainbow emote, cough, sleep via ForcedSleeping status), Healium (large Burn/Toxin/Brute heals plus sleep), Nitrium (tiered speed-boost by dose, matching "stronger the more you inhale"), Zauker (Slash/Heat/Poison damage-over-time, i.e. poison), reactions.yml defines gasReaction prototypes producing BZ, Healium, Nitrium, Pluoxium, HyperNoblium, Halon and a MetalHydrogenReaction gated on BZ+Hydrogen at low temperature; airalarms.yml adds threshold prototypes for the new gases; ElectrolyzerSystem.cs literally burns Plasma/Uranium sheet tags to convert WaterVapor into Hydrogen+Oxygen and separately consumes HyperNoblium/BZ; h2_fireaxe.yml is a Prying-tool fire axe described as "a lightweight crowbar ... fire axe head attached"; h2_armor.yml defines MetalHydrogenArmor/Helmet construction recipes. Going beyond the cited evidence, I fetched Content.Shared/Atmos/Atmospherics.cs (an upstream file, not under any _Funkystation directory) and found it directly edited: the shared `Gas` enum was extended from a smaller upstream set to include BZ=9 through AntiNoblium=19, `TotalNumberOfGases` was bumped to 20, and roughly a dozen molar/specific-heat constants carry "// Funky Atmos - /tg/ gases" comments marking in-place additions. This is stronger than the claim's own skip_because reasoning ("the shared gas enum have to grow") predicted — it isn't hypothetical, the fork's live copy already patches that upstream file directly, contradicting the drop-in seed outright.

**sunrise**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmospherics gets a much bigger periodic table to play with: BZ, healium, nitrium, pluoxium, hyper-noblium, halon, zauker, proto-nitrate, hydrogen, helium and Sunrise's own electrovae, each with its own colored haze you can see drifting through a room and its own chemical that enters your bloodstream if you breathe it. Making them is a puzzle of temperature and mixing rather than a machine you click: pluoxium wants a cold oxygen/CO2/tritium mix, nitrium needs 1500K, hyper-noblium only forms near absolute zero, and zauker demands a 50,000K furnace, so an atmos tech is chasing narrow temperature windows and can watch a batch decompose if the room drifts. The gases then do things in the world rather than just sitting in a canister: halon eats oxygen and smothers fires, hyper-noblium freezes other gas reactions dead, and charged electrovae throws lightning at whatever is standing on the tile until it discharges back to inert electrovae. Every gas carries a price per mole, so a good batch is also cargo revenue.

- **Take** — It turns atmospherics from "keep the air breathable" into a production discipline with its own recipes, hazards and export value, giving an under-served department hours of self-directed work.
- **Skip** — It cannot live in fork-only files: the gases have to be added to the shared Gas enum, the reactions sit in upstream Content.Server.Atmos namespaces, and electrovae adds a per-tile effect struct plus a new AtmosphereSystem method, so every upstream atmos change becomes a merge conflict you own forever.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, cargo, medical, science, all-players
- **Verified** — partly supported. Every substantive gameplay specific checked out in the files (gas list, pricePerMole and reagent fields, Pluoxium 50-273K O2/CO2/Tritium, Nitrium 1500K, HyperNoblium 2.7-15K, Zauker 50,000K, halon O2 absorption, the 5-mol hyper-noblium NoReaction guard in reaction classes, ApplyLightningStrike and ChargedElectrovae→Electrovae discharge); the only unsupported specific is the universal colored-haze claim, since Hydrogen and Helium lack overlay sprites. The needs-upstream-edits shape is confirmed as fork-patches-upstream: Content.Shared/Atmos/Atmospherics.cs contains "// Sunrise-edit" Gas enum entries 9-21 and Content.Server/Atmos/TileAtmosphere.cs contains "// Sunrise-Start/End" blocks, i.e. in-place edits to upstream files, plus fork files declared in upstream namespaces extending AtmosphereSystem via partial class.
  - unsupported: "each with its own colored haze you can see drifting through a room" — Hydrogen and Helium in gases.yml have no gasOverlaySprite/gasOverlayState, so those two gases are invisible; only the other gases have visible overlays.

**backmen**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmospherics techs get a whole second tier of gases to cook up in a burn chamber instead of just plasma, tritium and frezon: mixing the right gases at the right temperature makes BZ, pluoxium, nitrium, healium, halon, hyper-noblium, proto-nitrate and zauker, each with its own coloured cloud you can see through a window. They do very different things to whoever breathes them: healium knocks a person out and heals almost every kind of injury including poison and radiation, pluoxium is a super-efficient oxygen substitute that also scrubs CO2 out of your lungs, nitrium makes you sprint 50% faster and shrug off stuns before it starts poisoning you, BZ makes you see rainbows and rots you at the cellular level (and puts slimes to sleep), and halon smothers fires by eating oxygen. Each gas is also bottled as a throwable grenade and a canister, so the same chemistry shows up as medic tools, riot-control clouds and sabotage weapons rather than staying in the pipes.

- **Take** — It turns atmospherics from a maintenance chore into a production minigame with outputs other departments actually want (a healing gas for medical, a speed gas for security, a fire-suppression gas for engineering), and the gases arrive pre-packaged as grenades and canisters so the content is usable the moment it lands.
- **Skip** — The gases have to be registered in the engine's shared gas enum and threaded through scrubbers, air alarms, gas sensors, canister sprites and the English gas locale file, so it is a permanent set of upstream patches that will conflict on every atmos-touching upstream sync; several reaction chains (zauker needs 50,000 K) also only make sense if you have a supermatter-grade heat source, and none of it ships with guidebook pages, so players get a dozen new gases with no in-game explanation of what they do.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, medical, antagonist, cargo, science
- **Verified** — partly supported. The fork's own copy of Content.Shared/Atmos/Atmospherics.cs is edited in place (TotalNumberOfGases = 20 marked //ADT-Gas, Gas enum abbreviation entries for BZ/Pluoxium/Nitrium/Healium/HyperNoblium/ProtoNitrate/Zauker/Halon/Helium/AntiNoblium) and upstream Resources/Locale/en-US/atmos/gases.ftl carries the new abbreviations, so needs-upstream-edits is correct in the fork-patches-upstream sense. The gas/reaction/reagent/grenade/canister content itself checks out (including Zauker's 50,000 K reaction and healium/nitrium/BZ effects), but two specific gameplay statements are contradicted by the files.
  - unsupported: each with its own coloured cloud you can see through a window — gases.yml gives no gasOverlaySprite to BZ, Pluoxium, Hydrogen, or Helium (they render no cloud), and HyperNoblium reuses the frezon sprite state rather than its own
  - unsupported: pluoxium is a super-efficient oxygen substitute that also scrubs CO2 out of your lungs — the ModifyLungGas ratios are CarbonDioxide: 1.0, Pluoxium: -1.0 and ModifyLungGasEntityEffectSystem adds moles for positive ratios, so it converts pluoxium INTO lung CO2, the opposite direction of the claim

**goobstation**

*activity · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmospherics gets four new gases from SS13 that crew can actually manufacture, each with a distinct effect when breathed. BZ is a purple nerve gas that makes victims cough, see rainbows and choke on asphyxiation and poison damage; Healium is a pink gas that heals burns, brute and toxins while leaving the patient drunk and asleep; Nitrium is a brown stimulant gas that makes you run up to 60% faster at the price of stuttering and suffocation damage if you keep huffing it; Pluoxium breathes like a far denser oxygen, letting one canister keep people alive much longer, while poisoning Vox. Making them is the real content: an atmos tech cooks BZ from plasma and nitrous oxide below fire temperature, then uses BZ as a catalyst to spin nitrous oxide out of air, chill it with frezon into Healium, bake it with nitrogen and tritium above 1500K into Nitrium, or run a cold oxygen/CO2/tritium mix for Pluoxium. Nitrium also breaks back down in the presence of oxygen, so it has to be stored clean.

- **Take** — It turns atmospherics from a maintenance job into a production job with real payoffs the rest of the station wants: a healing gas for medical, a stimulant gas for anyone in a hurry, denser breathing gas for internals, and a knockout gas that antagonists will absolutely pipe into a department.
- **Skip** — Adding gases is not a drop-in: the gas list is an engine-side enum and every reaction's requirement array is index-ordered against it, so grafting this means editing upstream atmospherics in place, re-indexing any other fork reaction you already carry, and adding gas overlay sprites plus locale strings that this fork keeps outside the feature folder; the reagent definitions also carry Goob-specific conditions (Hydrakin, Cybernetic, Slime metabolizer types, a NitrosylPlasmide blood check) that must be stripped or stubbed or prototype validation will fail.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, medical, science, antagonist, all-players
- **Verified** — supported. Every specific gameplay number checks out in the fetched files (BZ 10 asphyx/2.5 poison + SeeingRainbows, Healium burn/brute/toxin heals + Drunk + ForcedSleep, Nitrium 1.25/1.4/1.6 speed tiers + Stutter + 15 asphyx, Pluoxium Oxygenate factor 8 + Vox Poison 10, and all six reactions with the claimed inputs and temperature bounds). Needs-upstream-edits is confirmed in both senses: the fork's copy of Content.Shared/Atmos/Atmospherics.cs adds Gas enum entries 9-12 and TotalNumberOfGases=13, and BZFormationReaction.cs lives in upstream Content.Server/Atmos/Reactions/ (200) not in a Goob namespace dir (404), so integrating elsewhere requires the same in-place upstream edits the skip_because describes.

**ephemeral-space**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Rot gas that builds up in a room now leaves a visible mark on the deck: the more of it pools on a tile, the more grime patches appear underfoot, so a body stashed in maintenance eventually stains the floor around it and the janitor (or anyone following the smell) can see where something has been decaying. There is also a new smoke gas that hangs in the air as a dark haze you can see and walk through, and air alarms are tuned to shriek at even a trace of it, so a fire or a chem mishap sets off the alarm well before the room is dangerous. Rushing air across a breach shows a brief puff of wind sprite that fades out instead of the air moving invisibly.

- **Take** — Turns an abstract gas number into something players can actually read off the floor and the alarm panel, which gives the janitor real work and makes hidden corpses and early fires visible without any new UI.
- **Skip** — The reaction calls into the fork's own filth/dirt-decal system through a fork-added `Miasma` member on the upstream AtmosphereSystem, so the three tidy prototype files are the small end of it, and the catalogue summary's promised roster (BZ, halon, healium, nitrium, etc.) is simply not present here, only smoke plus a miasma-to-grime reaction.
- **Touches** — service, engineering, atmospherics, all-players
- **Needs** — [laundry-and-stains](#laundry-and-stains)
- **Verified** — partly supported. I re-read all six cited files plus the fork's in-place upstream edits: ESMiasmaDirtReaction scales dirt decals with Miasma moles via a fork-added public ESMiasmaSystem Miasma dependency field inside upstream AtmosphereSystem.cs, ESSpaceWindEffect is spawned from ES-marked blocks in upstream HighPressureDelta.cs, Gas.Smoke=9 is added to the upstream Gas enum, and the reaction depends on Content.Server/_ES/Filth (the laundry-and-stains unit) — exactly the entanglement the claim describes. The gameplay gist holds (rotting bodies emit Gas.Miasma, fires release Smoke, sensors map Smoke to ESStationSmoke), but the alarm-sensitivity and chem-mishap specifics are not supported by the files.
  - unsupported: air alarms are tuned to shriek at even a trace of it — ESStationSmoke's danger threshold is 0.05 mole fraction (warn at 0.5x that), identical to the existing stationMiasma threshold and 10x looser than stationCO2 (0.006) or stationPlasma (0.005); nothing in the files shows trace-level tuning
  - unsupported: a fire or a chem mishap sets off the alarm — fires are supported (FlammableSystem releases Gas.Smoke), but no chem reaction anywhere in the repo produces the Smoke gas; the chem-mishap path is not in the files

**monolith**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Atmos gains extra gases beyond the standard roster (BZ, healium, nitrium alongside frezon) and a crystallizer machine that turns a carefully tuned gas mix held at a narrow temperature band into solid goods: plastic, plasteel, plastitanium, iridite, plasma sheets, diamonds, even telecrystals. Getting a recipe right means hitting both the gas ratios and a temperature window (sometimes tens of thousands of kelvin), and the hotter recipes are flagged dangerous, so a botched run is an explosion rather than a wasted batch. Frezon-rich gas deposits exist out in the world to be tapped as feedstock, and a storage-chamber pressure alarm nags when the holding tank creeps toward its limit. Separately, temperature becomes something you feel: heaters warm everyone within a couple of tiles up to a capped comfortable temperature, sitting in a given temperature band applies effects to your body, and hugging or petting another crewmate actually shares body heat between you, so a warm person can thaw out someone who came in from the cold.

- **Take** — It gives atmospherics a real production output — high-value sheets, diamonds and telecrystals gated behind precise gas-and-temperature work — instead of atmos existing only to keep the air breathable.
- **Skip** — The gas roster extension means editing the upstream gas enum and every mixture table that indexes it, and the heat-exchange code reads a coefficient added to upstream's TemperatureComponent, so this cannot land without touching core atmos and temperature files that every future upstream merge will conflict with.
- **Touches** — engineering, atmospherics, cargo, salvage, science, medical, all-players
- **Needs** — [atmospherics-simulation](#atmospherics-simulation)
- **Verified** — partly supported. I re-fetched all six cited files plus the fork's Atmospherics.cs and TemperatureComponent.cs: the gas enum is edited in place to 13 gases (BZ/Healium/Nitrium/Pluoxium, marked //Funky/Goob) and InteractionExchangeCoefficient is added to upstream's TemperatureComponent (absent in wizden master), so needs-upstream-edits is confirmed under the fork-patches-upstream reading, and grafting elsewhere would require the same core-file edits (hence both). Gameplay is accurate except the explosion consequence, which no code implements.
  - unsupported: "the hotter recipes are flagged dangerous, so a botched run is an explosion rather than a wasted batch" — the dangerous flag exists in the recipe YAML and in CrystallizerRecipePrototype.cs, but nothing consumes it: the 363-line CrystallizerSystem.cs, CrystallizerComponent.cs, CrystallizerWindow.xaml.cs, and CrystallizerBoundUserInterface.cs contain zero references to 'dangerous' and no explosion logic; the only in-code consequence of a recipe is EnergyRelease heating/cooling the internal gas mix.

## Cargo

### Vending machines and container fills

<a id="vending-and-loot-catalogs"></a>

Fork-specific vending machine rosters with their own inventories and ad barks, plus the crate, locker, box and maintenance loot tables that stock the station.

**ephemeral-space**

*activity · entangled · medium confidence · 11 forks carry it*

Adds a set of station-flavour vending machines with their own product lists and chatty ad barks, including a pride-clothing "Gender Bash" dispenser, a scrap-dealer machine hawking military and engineering surplus scavenged from derelicts, a plain water cooler, and a lumber-and-welding-fuel machine that warns you not to light fires indoors. Their stock is rolled from loot tables at round start rather than being a fixed shelf, so two rounds of the same machine can offer different goods. It also rewrites what you find when you crack open lockers, dressers, wardrobes, backpacks, briefcases and maintenance crates: theatre wardrobes hand out whole themed costumes (jester, chicken suit, cowboy in one of three colours, shrine maiden, Santa), and there are hidden ceiling caches that spawn a matched antagonist kit, for example a silenced pistol with spare magazine, sleepy pens and smoke grenades, or a chameleon backpack with an emag and C4.

- **Take** — Randomised, table-driven vending stock plus themed costume and cache fills is a cheap way to make looting and maintenance scavenging feel different every round, and the machinery for it is a single small fork-namespaced component and system.
- **Skip** — The content is only as good as the entity ids it points at: the traitor caches pull in the fork's own guns, magazines, emag and chameleon backpack and its assassin/infiltrator/marauder/subverter archetypes, so those tables need rewriting against your own item set, and the vending machines are inert until someone places them on your maps.
- **Touches** — cargo, service, salvage, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [firearms-content-expansion](#firearms-content-expansion), [traitor-uplink](#traitor-uplink)
- **Verified** — partly supported; assessed as drop-in, corrected by the verifier. All files I fetched are namespace-contained under _ES (and DatasetVocalizer at Content.Server/Vocalization exists identically in wizden upstream, so no fork patch), but the vendor prototypes in vending.yml embed ESAmbientMusicMarker* components from the fork's ambient-music unit and a fork guidebook ref, the cache tables reference ES weapons/emag/uplink items, and the headline ceiling-cache gameplay only exists via the fork's SecretIdentity system — two undisclosed cross-unit dependencies beyond the two listed in depends_on.
  - unsupported: "a plain water cooler" — the machine is a 'suspicious water vendor' ('It looks normal...?') whose fill rolls half its stock from SuspiciousWaterTable; it is a trap/joke vendor, not a plain water dispenser
  - unsupported: "so two rounds of the same machine can offer different goods" — true for Salvaged Sam's, the water vendor, and Woodgrain (prob/rolls selectors), but the Gender Bash vendor's inventory is a fixed all-selector with fixed amounts and is identical every round
  - unsupported: "there are hidden ceiling caches that spawn a matched antagonist kit" — this unit's caches.yml only defines ordinary CrateGenericSteel prototypes and loot tables (the 'hidden in the ceiling' description is commented out); the ceiling-hiding, reveal alert, and mind-matched spawning are implemented in Content.Shared/_ES/SecretIdentity/Traitor/ (ESCeilingCacheComponent, ESSecretIdentityCacheSpawnerComponent), a separate system depending on _ES/Core/Timer, _ES/SpawnRegion and an Alert prototype, none of which is in this unit or its depends_on list; the caches also include crew archetypes (mercenary, insider, arms dealer, hitman), not only antagonist kits
  - unsupported: "the machinery for it is a single small fork-namespaced component and system" — true only for vendor fills; locker/crate/cache fills use upstream EntityTableContainerFill, and the cache-delivery machinery is a separate multi-file SecretIdentity system

**impstation**

*activity · needs upstream edits · high confidence · 11 forks carry it*

Impstation stocks the station with its own branded vending machines that chatter their own ad barks: a sports-drink cooler pushing "healthy bodies, healthy bottom line", a Discount Dan's shrimp-snack machine that warns you the snacks "cannot and will not smile back", a juice machine that nags you about booze, a salvage wardrobe selling mining gear with jokes about fresh-looking corpses, and Deadly Dan's, which sells a cheap disposable pistol and hidden ammo behind lines like "Safety feature? The point is to be Deadly!" Each machine also has a wire-hacked contraband row (a sniper rifle, a clown mask, a cigarette pack) so breaking one open is worth doing. Alongside that, the crates cargo can order and the lockers, closets and boxes scattered around the map are refilled from fork tables, so you order a princess dress-up crate, a theremin, a drum kit or a box of 500 cigarettes, and the wall closets you pry open hold the fork's own uniform and clothing sets rather than the stock ones.

- **Take** — It is the cheapest possible way to make a station feel like your fork: pure YAML lists of items, ad lines and prices, no C# at all, and you can adopt one vendor or one crate at a time.
- **Skip** — Almost every entry points at Impstation's own items (shrimp snacks, Asterade sodas, the Valu pistol, fork instruments and plushies), so a naive copy yields a wall of missing-prototype errors unless you first port the content those lists sell; and several fill files reuse upstream prototype ids like ClosetWallAtmosphericsFilled, which collides with the upstream definitions rather than sitting quietly beside them.
- **Touches** — cargo, salvage, service, security, all-players
- **Needs** — [food-and-drink-expansion](#food-and-drink-expansion), [clothing-and-wardrobe-pack](#clothing-and-wardrobe-pack), [firearms-content-expansion](#firearms-content-expansion), [object-and-tool-art](#object-and-tool-art)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 11 forks carry it*

Instead of a machine that spits out one item per click, RMC-14's vendors open a shop window with named tabs (Ammo, Smokables, Radio Keys, Kits) where every item carries a price and you spend a personal budget that the machine remembers per person. Vendors only unlock for the right job or rank, some categories let you take exactly one thing and then close for you, some hand you a pre-filled box instead of a loose item, and squad specialist kits are limited server-wide so once two people have claimed a kit nobody else can. Buying certain kits also rewrites your visible role title, name prefix and map marker, so the crew can see what you picked. Points can trickle back over time on some machines, a machine with the right engineering know-how can be hacked open after a short delay to bypass its access lock, and anyone can top a machine back up by feeding it spare stock.

- **Take** — Turns "walk to vending machine, click item" into a real per-character equipment budget with scarcity, role gating and public role identity, which gives every department a meaningful loadout decision at round start instead of a free-for-all grab.
- **Skip** — The vendor gating is wired straight into RMC-14's rank, skill and squad systems (rank lists, an engineering skill level for hacking, squad mapping, global specialist-kit caps), so grafting it means either importing those systems or rewriting every gate, and then hand-authoring an entire priced catalogue per machine before a single one is usable.
- **Touches** — all-players, command, security, medical, cargo, service
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [loadout-system](#loadout-system)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Expedition missions and objectives

<a id="expedition-missions"></a>

Structured off-station runs with typed objectives (capture, rescue, purge, shutdown), mission consoles, rewards and deployable shelters.

**deltav**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Salvagers earn Mining Points by feeding ore into the station's ore processor and smelting it, then tap their ID card on the processor to bank the points to themselves. Those points are the currency for going back out: firing the salvage magnet to haul in another wreck costs a listed number of Mining Points, so the shift becomes a self-funding loop of drag ore home, cash it in, buy the next wreck. Miners also get a voucher they redeem at a vendor for exactly one loadout of their choosing (a kinetic crusher kit, a fulton extraction-and-rescue kit that lets them ship ore and dead bodies home without the shuttle, a survival kit with explorer's webbing and a holoflare gun, a crusher-dagger small arms kit, or a conscription kit that outfits a second crewmember as a miner). Out in the field they can throw down a shelter capsule, which shakes, warns everyone to stand back, and unfolds into a small prefab room on solid ground for a pressurised place to retreat to.

- **Take** — It turns salvage from an open-ended "go get stuff" chore into a closed economic loop where the department funds its own next expedition, which gives miners a concrete score to push and a reason to keep processing ore rather than hoarding it.
- **Skip** — Mining Points are not self-contained: the earning side is bolted onto the upstream lathe (a new recipe field, a print event hook, and a "claim points" button in the lathe UI) and the spending side patches the salvage magnet and ID card prototypes, so grafting it means touching upstream files that will conflict on every sync. The voucher also rides on the thief-backpack set prototype, and several advertised kits (resonator, minebot) are commented-out TODOs upstream in Delta-V itself.
- **Touches** — cargo, salvage, service
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**frontier**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

A crew flies their ship out to open space, uses the salvage console aboard, and picks one of five offered planetside missions, each listing the enemy faction, a time limit, and hazard modifiers like night time, extreme cold, no breathable air, plasma atmosphere, or a rushed clock. The ship jumps to a planet, everyone gets pointed toward an objective building, and they dig and mine through rock, pry open airlocks, fight cultists, xenos, mercenaries or rogue silicons, and haul back ore and loot before the ship jumps home with or without them. Loadout planning is the real game: the console's modifier readout tells you whether to bring a hardsuit, a winter coat, flares, medical supplies, or armour matched to the enemy's damage type, and hostile mobs that chase you off the dungeon grid are killed off so you cannot drag the planet's wildlife home.

- **Take** — It turns salvage from a side errand into a full prep-fight-extract loop with a timer, hazard readouts and enemy variety, giving a whole crew a reason to gear up, coordinate on radio and go somewhere together.
- **Skip** — Frontier's version is built for its ship-owning sector economy (ships bought at the Expeditionary Lodge, telecomms servers aboard, no station radio planetside) and hooks partial-class extensions into upstream mining and salvage systems, so a station-based fork inherits a lot of surrounding assumptions plus ongoing merge pain on upstream salvage files.
- **Touches** — cargo, salvage, mining, medical, security, all-players
- **Needs** — [shipyard-player-ships](#shipyard-player-ships), [hostile-fauna-and-npcs](#hostile-fauna-and-npcs)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

## Machines

### Access control rework

<a id="access-control-rework"></a>

Changes how doors and equipment check identity: rebuilt access sets, scan gates, secure terminals, keypad locks, grid ownership and ID-gated item use.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Command staff work an ID terminal that first makes them badge in with their own card, then lets them slot a crewmate's card and re-badge it wholesale: pick a job, and the card instantly gets that role's entire door-and-locker set (UNMC Command, Medical, Engineering, Military Police, Requisitions, the marine squad roles, plus colony and corporate sets), tick individual accesses on top, post someone to a squad, flip their friend-or-foe tag, or "terminate" a card so it stops reading as friendly. Every one of those edits is written into the ship AI's public identification log, so a quiet promotion or a stripped card can be read back later by anyone who checks. Because access is bundled per role, your card is also what decides which equipment vendors will hand you gear, not just which doors open, and wall-mounted keycard stations need two people to swipe authorized cards at separate posts within a short window of each other before the ship will go to Red Alert.

- **Take** — Bundling access into named role groups turns ID editing from tag-by-tag clicking into one meaningful command decision, and the two-person keycard turn plus the public AI log give access changes real social weight.
- **Skip** — It is not a standalone access system: the console reaches into that fork's squad assignment, rank, skill, IFF faction and ship-AI logging systems, and the group tree itself is written around a marine job roster, so porting it means either dragging those in or rewriting most of the prototypes for your own jobs.
- **Touches** — command, security, medical, engineering, cargo, all-players
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster), [firearms-content-expansion](#firearms-content-expansion)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**serbia-strong**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

Some doors only open if you approach them from the right side, so a checkpoint airlock lets people out freely but refuses anyone pushing in from the wrong direction, and certain doors can be gated on what you *are* rather than what your ID says, letting a door open for a borg or a specific faction while rejecting a stolen card. Certain items refuse to be picked up, worn or dragged by the wrong person at all: grab a weapon or piece of gear you're not cleared for and it slips out of your hands with a warning message, a buzz, and in some cases a shock that hurts you for trying. There is also a painter machine with a slot for a PDA and a slot for an ID card that reskins them to look like a different department's, so you can walk around presenting as someone you aren't, and the ID access list itself is expanded with extra levels like Psychologist, Interdyne and USSP for fork-specific factions and jobs.

- **Take** — Item-level ID restriction is the piece upstream genuinely lacks: it stops gear theft at the point of pickup instead of relying on players to respect job boundaries, and it gives security a real reason that a stolen ID is not a master key.
- **Skip** — The pieces are not self-contained: directional access has to be consulted from upstream's access reader, and the whitelist door check listens for a fork-only door event that upstream door code must be patched to raise, so grafting it means carrying permanent edits in upstream files that will conflict on every sync. The access-level prototypes are also tied to SS220's own factions (Interdyne, USSP) and are dead weight without them.
- **Touches** — security, command, medical, engineering, service, antagonist, all-players
- **Verified** — supported. I cloned the fork and grepped it directly. Content.Shared/Doors/Systems/SharedDoorSystem.cs (an upstream file, not under any SS220 namespace) is patched in place inside HasAccess(): it raises a fork-only DoorAccesAttemptEvent (consumed by Content.Shared/SS220/AccessWhitelist/SharedAccessWhitelistSystem.cs) and calls the fork's DirectionalAccessSystem.IsDirectionAllowed, both wrapped in '// SS220-directional-access' / '//SS220 ChameleonStructure' comment markers. That is exactly the 'listens for a fork-only door event that upstream door code must be patched to raise' claim in skip_because, and it is a real in-place edit to an upstream file, not just a hypothetical integration cost. The item-restriction (SharedRestrictedItemSystem.cs: cancels BeingEquippedAttemptEvent/BeingPulledAttemptEvent/GettingPickedUpAttemptEvent, popup + damage + sound on fail), the PDA/ID painter (PdaIdPainterComponent with IdCardSlot/PdaSlot and Chosen/Default EntProtoId fields, self-contained), and the extra access levels (Psychologist in medical.yml, Interdyne in interdyne.yml + access.ftl, USSP in ussp.yml) all read exactly as described.

**ephemeral-space**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

The armory no longer opens with one ID: three separate hand-scanner switches ("Casper", "Melchior", "Yalthasar") are scattered around the control room and three different people must each flip one inside a short window, after which a station announcement counts down twenty seconds and the gates open. Get it wrong or leave one unpressed and everyone in the control room is shocked, an announcement calls out trespassers, and the switches lock out for a cooldown. Elsewhere, doors carry a printed emergency access code you can read by examining them, and an Emergency Access Terminal lets someone type that code in to look up a specific airlock and see whether it is bolted, powered and on emergency access, then toggle emergency access remotely. Lockers, doors and built objects can also be secured with a numeric keypad you punch a PIN into (and rewire with a tool to change the code), while a physical jammer item can be wedged into a door to keep it bolted shut until someone pulls it out.

- **Take** — The three-person armory switch turns "sec opens the gun room" into a public, announced, co-signed ritual that other players can hear, contest or sabotage, which is a real shift in how weapons enter a round rather than a new toy.
- **Skip** — The armory sequence assumes a map that actually has a control room with the switches placed in it, so it does nothing on your existing stations until you re-map them, and the keypad, emergency-access and emag pieces reach into upstream airlock, locker and construction prototypes rather than sitting neatly in fork files.
- **Touches** — security, command, engineering, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [shared-utility-libraries](#shared-utility-libraries), [custom-station-maps](#custom-station-maps)
- **Verified** — partly supported. I cloned the repo and confirmed the core claims: the three named switches (Casper/Melchior/Yalthasar), the 20-second ArmoryOpenDelay, the 2.5s ButtonPressAllTimeframe window, the electrocution+announcement+2-minute-cooldown failure path, the emergency-access door examine text/terminal UI strings, the keypad PIN/rewire-with-tool mechanic, and the door jammer bolt-while-embedded behavior are all directly backed by the cited files. But the claimed 'needs-upstream-edits' downgrade from the seed's 'drop-in' guess is also independently confirmed: Resources/Prototypes/Entities/Structures/Doors/Airlocks/base_structureairlocks.yml (a vanilla-namespaced file, not under _ES/) is edited in place with an '# ES START'/'# ES END' block adding `ESEmergencyAccessDoor` directly onto the base airlock entity. That is a real in-place upstream edit shipped in the fork's own tree (fork-patches-upstream), and replicating the feature elsewhere would require the same edit to that codebase's own base airlock file (integration-would-require-upstream-edits) — both readings hold. Separately, I found the 'Lockers ... can be secured with a keypad' clause is not actually wired anywhere in the codebase (no Storage/Closets prototype has a DeviceLinkSink or keypad reference), so I flagged it as an overclaim.
  - unsupported: "Lockers, doors and built objects can also be secured with a numeric keypad you punch a PIN into" — the keypad only works by invoking a DeviceLink source port (ESKeypadLock/ESKeypadUnlock) against a target's DeviceLinkSinkComponent; only the base airlock prototype was patched with DeviceLinkSink-capable wiring for the fork's door work, and no locker/closet prototype in Resources/Prototypes/Entities/Structures/Storage/ has a DeviceLinkSinkComponent or any keypad-related addition. Storage lockers structurally cannot be wired to a keypad in this codebase as written.

**sunrise**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 6 forks carry it*

When the station changes alert level, an announcement warns that door permissions are being rewritten, and a short time later a second announcement confirms it: on red or gamma, security officers and the detective can suddenly open armory and security doors they could not before; on yellow, engineers get atmospherics. The same bundle adds walk-through scanner arches that ping and flash green or red as you pass and can be wired to doors or alarms so contraband trips a checkpoint, plus gear that simply refuses to work for the wrong hands — a nuke op rifle pops "refused" if a crewman picks it up, a hardsuit can be DNA-keyed to its owner, and some items check your tags before you can use them at all. There is also a powered "pacificator" machine that, while running, stops everyone within a wide radius from attacking anyone.

- **Take** — Alert levels stop being flavour text and start physically redistributing who can open which doors, which gives security escalation real mechanical teeth and gives scan-gate checkpoints something to enforce.
- **Skip** — It is not one feature but seven loosely related ones (alert access, scan gates, faction biocode, DNA-locked suits, tag restrictions, faction gear blockers, pacificator field) that happen to share a folder, so you inherit the whole maintenance surface even if you only wanted the alert-driven access.
- **Touches** — security, command, engineering, antagonist, all-players, cargo
- **Needs** — [abductors](#abductors), [modular-hardsuits](#modular-hardsuits)
- **Verified** — supported. I refetched all 13 cited files and they solidly back the gameplay description: ExtendedAccessSystem.cs (Content.Server/_Sunrise/ExtendedAccess) confirms the two-stage announcement-then-apply flow on AlertLevelChangedEvent; access_group.yml confirms Red/Gamma grant Security+Armory+Detective and Yellow grants ChiefEngineer+Atmospherics; ScanGateComponent.cs confirms scan-gate arches with success/fail states, sounds, and wireable SuccessSignal/FailSignal; BiocodeComponent/System confirm faction-gated item refusal with a popup; PersonalBiocodeComponent confirms a DNA field explicitly commented '// for now only for modsuits'; RestrictByUserTagComponent confirms tag-gated item use; PacificatorComponent/Systems confirm a powered, rangeable (32f) field that adds PacifiedComponent to nearby humanoids. Critically for graft_shape, I went beyond the cited paths and pulled Content.Shared/Access/Components/AccessReaderComponent.cs, Content.Shared/Access/Systems/AccessReaderSystem.cs, and Content.Server/AlertLevel/AlertLevelPrototype.cs — all outside any _Sunrise namespace directory — and each contains explicit '// Sunrise added' edits (the AlertAccesses dictionary field, the UpdateAccess method, and the ExtendedAccessOptions field respectively) that this feature's own _Sunrise code calls into. That is definitive proof the fork's own copy patches upstream files in place (fork-patches-upstream), and separately, grafting this into another codebase would require the same upstream edits (integration-would-require-upstream-edits) — so both readings hold. I also found RestrictByUserTagComponent.cs directly imports and is Access-restricted to Content.Shared._Sunrise.Antags.Abductor.SharedAbductorSystem, which independently confirms the claim's depends_on abductors is a real code coupling, not speculation.
  - unsupported: "a nuke op rifle pops \"refused\" if a crewman picks it up" — I confirmed the underlying mechanism (BiocodeComponent checks NpcFactionMemberComponent and pops a popup using AlertText, default "item-biocode-refused", cancelling the action) in Content.Shared/_Sunrise/Biocode/BiocodeComponent.cs and BiocodeSystem.cs, but could not locate the actual nuke-ops rifle prototype file to confirm this specific item uses the component; it's a plausible but unverified illustrative example.
  - unsupported: touches: "cargo" is not supported by any of the cited evidence files — none of them reference cargo, mining, or quartermaster mechanics; the only hit anywhere was an unrelated code comment ('// Sec owned by cargo.') in AccessReaderSystem.cs.

### Additional devices, gadgets and structures

<a id="device-and-machine-additions"></a>

Assorted new station hardware: handheld gadgets, device-linking hardware, holoprojectors, text screens, display cases, genetics consoles, sensor towers and soul-binding golems.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · high confidence · 15 forks carry it*

Instead of radio just working, players have to build and maintain the infrastructure that carries their voices. On the ground, comms towers and a sensor tower start broken: engineers weld the panels shut, cut and repair wiring, wrench the mast straight, restore power to the local APC, then tune the tower with an access tuner to add their side's radio channels — until that's done, nobody groundside can reach the ship, and the sensor tower feeds live enemy positions to the command map while it lasts (it randomly breaks again and needs re-repair). Where radio doesn't reach, players use a telephone network: rotary phones and wall phones are named after their location, you pick a destination off a tabbed list, the far phone audibly rings and emits a "starts ringing vigorously" emote, and whoever picks up hears you in a distinct purple anonymous whisper as long as you both keep the handset in hand and stay close. Command also gets an AI terminal you swipe your ID into to browse timestamped round logs by category, plus megaphones, a hand labeler, and squad-broadcast consoles that push announcements and map drawings to everyone.

- **Take** — The phone network and repairable comms tower turn communication itself into a contested objective — a genuinely fresh engineering job and a reason for command to leave the console — and the phone UX (named handsets, ringing emote, anonymous purple speech, hang-up on walking away) is a polished, self-contained interaction most forks have no equivalent of.
- **Skip** — RMC-14 is a total-conversion Aliens fork and this hardware is written against its own faction/IFF, access, squad and tactical-map stack; the ARES terminal in particular reads ID cards through RMC's faction system and pulls from an RMC-specific round-log pipeline, so porting it means either dragging those systems along or rewriting the plumbing, and the whole design assumes a two-site round (ship plus planet) where cutting radio is meaningful rather than just annoying.
- **Touches** — command, engineering, security, service, all-players
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [access-control-rework](#access-control-rework), [hud-and-status-readouts](#hud-and-status-readouts)
- **Verified** — partly supported. Fetched all three cited files directly. The two guidebook XMLs confirm nearly every specific gameplay beat: tower repair steps (weld panels, wire cutters, wrench, power, then Security Access Tuner to add faction frequencies), radio dead until repaired, sensor tower tracking unknown biosignatures on TACMAP and randomly breaking down needing re-repair, and the telephone system's named handsets, \"starts ringing vigorously\" emote, purple anonymous local-chat whisper while both hold the phone, and auto-hangup on walking away — all verbatim-matched. ARESExternalTerminalSystem.cs (plus a follow-up fetch of ARESCoreSystem.cs) confirms the ID-card login, category-filtered logs, and timestamped entries (\"dd - HH:mm\" format via CreateARESLog), and crucially confirms the entangled call: the terminal directly calls GunIFFSystem.HasFaction and reads Content.Shared._RMC14.Access's AccessComponent, i.e. it is wired into RMC14's own faction/IFF and access subsystems even though those live under the same _RMC14 top-level directory the path-scan treats as \"in namespace\" — exactly the kind of internal entanglement the claim used to downgrade the drop-in seed. That downgrade is correct and evidence-backed. Two flavor items (megaphones, hand labeler) in the gameplay prose have no support in the cited evidence or elsewhere I could check.
  - unsupported: "plus megaphones, a hand labeler, and squad-broadcast consoles that push announcements and map drawings to everyone" — the squad-broadcast/announcement/map-drawing part is backed by RMCGuideMarineCommunications.xml (Overwatch Console's Message Squad button, Groundside Operations Console announcements, Tactical Map Canvas-tab drawing broadcast to all marines), but megaphones and a hand labeler appear nowhere in the three cited evidence files and I could not locate them elsewhere (GitHub code search requires auth from this sandbox), so those two specific items are unverified.

**sunrise**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 15 forks carry it*

A grab-bag of new station hardware. The standout is the interrogator: security drags a cuffed or dead suspect into a powered upright machine, waits about thirty seconds of humming, and a ding announces that every removable implant has been pulled out of them, so a captured traitor loses their uplink and freedom implants without a surgeon. Alongside it are courtroom gavels that bang with a loud crack and shatter if you keep hammering, keypad doors and lockers that ask for a numeric code and beep angrily at a wrong one, cabinets that seal shut once you put something inside so only an emag reopens them, wall loudspeakers, disposal auto-loaders that batch items into one pipe cycle, admin-only hand teleporters that make cross-map or ghost-only portals, and a pile of new console circuit boards (cargo finance, station bank, salary, bounty sale, ERT call and approve, security fine records) so those consoles can be built and repaired rather than only mapped in.

- **Take** — Mostly additive hardware you can cherry-pick one item at a time, and the interrogator alone gives security a real, non-surgical answer to implanted antagonists that upstream lacks.
- **Skip** — It is a grab-bag rather than a feature, so half of it (finance, salary, bank, bounty-sale and ERT boards) is dead weight unless you also run Sunrise's economy and ERT systems, and several pieces sit in upstream namespaces (the disposal auto-loader and the sealing cabinet) so they need upstream call sites patched and will re-conflict on every sync.
- **Touches** — security, engineering, cargo, command, service, antagonist, admins
- **Needs** — [player-economy-and-banking](#player-economy-and-banking), [cargo-bounties-and-market](#cargo-bounties-and-market)
- **Verified** — partly supported. The claim's needs-upstream-edits classification is directly confirmed: Content.Server/Disposal/Unit/DisposableSystem.cs (a genuine upstream-path file, no _Sunrise markers) was edited in place to call the fork's AutoLoaderSystem.Cycle() at lines 90-127, a real in-place upstream patch, not a hypothetical wiring concern. Most individual devices (interrogator, gavel, keypad console, wall loudspeaker board, admin hand teleporters, and all the named console circuit boards) are confirmed present and self-contained additive prototypes/systems. But the flagship interrogator mechanic is mis-described: the code aborts extraction and immediately ejects the body the instant MobStateSystem.IsDead() is true (InterrogatorSystem.cs Update loop), so a suspect who is already dead, or who dies mid-extraction, never gets EjectImplants() called on them - the claim's premise of using it on a 'dead suspect' is contradicted by the code, and there is also no cuffed-status check anywhere (CanDropTargetEvent only checks HasComp&lt;BodyComponent&gt;).
  - unsupported: security drags a cuffed or dead suspect into a powered upright machine, waits about thirty seconds of humming, and a ding announces that every removable implant has been pulled out of them
  - unsupported: several pieces sit in upstream namespaces (the disposal auto-loader and the sealing cabinet) so they need upstream call sites patched

### Stains and laundry

<a id="laundry-and-stains"></a>

Spilled reagents and blood leave persistent stains on clothing, walls and floors that must be scrubbed, wrung out or washed in a washing machine.

**forky, gaby-station-now-dumont-station, backmen, goobstation**

*activity · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Spilled liquids no longer vanish: blood, chemicals and drinks soak into your clothes as visible splatter overlays, smear onto walls, and track behind you as fading footprints wherever you walked through a puddle. You can wring a soaked garment out by hand (a slow, ~15-second job that dumps the liquid on the floor at your feet), scrub a wall stain off with a wetted rag while a dry one just smears it, or stuff up to four items into an industrial washing machine, shut the door and run a cycle — the drum sprays water, thumps loudly, and anyone who climbs in with the laundry gets beaten up by it. Stains stay scannable for DNA, so a bloodstained jumpsuit or a trail of footprints is real evidence a detective can follow, and some outer layers keep the clothing underneath clean. Forky also lets flammable spills soaked into a wall catch fire; other forks ship narrower slices (Backmen adds showers and washing machines, Goob just the footprints).

- **Take** — Turns the station's mess into something that persists and has to be dealt with, giving the janitor real work and giving security a physical evidence trail (bloody footprints, DNA-scannable stains) that survives the moment of the crime.
- **Skip** — Very asset- and hook-heavy for what is mostly flavor: every stainable garment needs per-slot stain overlay layers authored, and the spill/footprint hooks sit in upstream fluid and movement code, so it re-breaks on every upstream sync and adds constant entity churn (a stain entity per splatter) to already busy rounds.
- **Touches** — service, security, medical, all-players, janitorial
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**ephemeral-space, funky**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 5 forks carry it*

Spills on the floor stop looking like one generic coloured blob. Every liquid gets its own mess style: blood lands as scattered droplets that grow into ragged pools, water and shiny liquids look thin and wet, acid looks caustic and irritating, powders look dry and dusty, and vomit, pie filling and broken eggs each have their own hand-drawn look that ignores the usual tinting. Puddles pick randomly from several small, medium and large variants, so a medbay floor after a bad shift or a chem spill in the hallway reads at a glance as blood versus chemicals versus water instead of everyone squinting at colour alone. This is purely how spills look; mopping, slipping and reagent behaviour work exactly as before. Note that despite the catalogue name, nothing here was found for clothing stains, scrubbing or washing machines, and the Funky Station side of this unit lists no files at all.

- **Take** — Cheap, self-contained visual upgrade that makes floor spills instantly readable as blood, chems, water or vomit instead of identical recoloured blobs.
- **Skip** — It needs new fields bolted onto the upstream reagent prototype plus a whole new puddle sprite sheet, and it is mislabelled here: it delivers puddle variety, not the laundry or clothing-stain system the catalogue name promises.
- **Touches** — all-players, medical, service, science, janitorial
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

## Ui

### HUD status readouts and alerts

<a id="hud-and-status-readouts"></a>

Reworks the on-screen status layer with new alert widgets, resource bars, ammo counters, job and antag status icons, and richer speech bubbles.

**rmc14**

*loop-change · entangled · high confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Everyone gets a persistent on-screen compass arrow that points toward whoever matters to them: marines track their squad leader (or the captain, the landing zone, the corporate liaison), while xenos track the queen, hive leaders, tunnels and resin markers, with the arrow swinging as you move. A shared tactical map opens from a screen button, showing live blips for your side over the station layout, and leaders can draw coloured lines and drop text labels on it that everyone on their side then sees. Command staff sit at an Overwatch console listing every marine in a squad with their status (conscious, unconscious, dead, SSD), role, location, distance from the squad leader and a saved comment, from which they can broadcast squad-wide messages, set squad objectives, hide or promote people, watch through helmet cameras, and call in supply drops or orbital bombardment. Medics see floating health icons over bodies that distinguish "still defibbable", "nearly too late" and "permanently gone", plus a holocard tag they can set on a patient to flag their treatment status to the next doctor. Senior officers can award named medals with written citations, and anyone can recommend a crewmate for one, with the recommendations queueing up for approval on a medals panel.

- **Take** — It turns "where is everyone and how bad off are they" from radio guesswork into a readable shared picture, which is exactly what command and medical roles lack and the single biggest lever on coordinated play.
- **Skip** — Almost none of it stands alone: the trackers, Overwatch console and medical icons are readouts for RMC-14's squad roster, hive structure and its multi-stage defib window, so grafting the UI without those underlying systems leaves widgets with nothing to display.
- **Touches** — command, medical, security, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [squads-ranks-and-skills](#squads-ranks-and-skills), [xenomorph-hive](#xenomorph-hive), [resuscitation-and-life-support](#resuscitation-and-life-support)
- **Verified** — supported. Every specific in the gameplay text checks out against the raw files. alerts.yml contains the full tracker family: HiveTracker/LeaderTracker/TunnelTracker/HiveCoreTracker/ResinMarkerTracker for xenos, and a long SquadTracker* list (per-squad leaders, CommandingOfficer, ExecutiveOfficer, ChiefMilitaryPolice, LandingZone, CorporateLiaison) for marines, each with directional trackon-north/south/etc. sprite states matching a swinging compass arrow. overwatch.ftl has every named feature: Name/Role/State(conscious/unconscious/dead/SSD)/Location/SL Dist. columns, COMMENT + Save Comment, message-squad, squad-objectives, supply-drop, orbital-bombardment (warhead/crate/cooldown strings), hide/show-marine, promote-squad-leader, and a NO CAMERA string implying camera viewing. tacmap.ftl confirms colored line drawing (7 colors, line width) and a label create/edit/delete dialog. medals.ftl confirms citation prompts, award recommendations, and a medals panel with approve/reject tabs. holocard.ftl gives exact treatment-status states (Surgery/Emergency/Permadead/Xeno Embryo) matching the 'flag treatment status' claim precisely. CMHealthIconsSystem.cs directly imports Content.Shared._RMC14.Medical.Unrevivable and computes DeadDefib/DeadClose/DeadAlmost/DeadDNR/Dead stages, confirming the multi-stage defib dependency the claim's skip_because cites. On graft_shape: all files I fetched (including CMHealthIconsSystem's imports) stay inside _RMC14 namespaces, so the seed's 'zero files outside fork namespace' is literally true, but the claim's 'entangled' verdict is about functional coupling to the fork's OTHER units (squad roster/ranks, hive structure, defib stage system), not about upstream file patching — and that coupling is directly visible in the code (CMHealthIconsSystem depends on RMCUnrevivableSystem; SquadTracker alerts are meaningless without the squad system; HiveTracker alerts are meaningless without hive leadership). That supports downgrading the seed's drop-in to entangled, exactly as the claim argues.

**serbia-strong**

*cosmetic · entangled · medium confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Players wearing the right eyewear see a much denser layer of floating icons above people's heads: a security HUD that distinguishes wanted, under surveillance, to-be-searched, paroled, incarcerated, demoted, discharged and marked-for-execution instead of just a couple of criminal flags, job badges for the fork's extra roles (magistrate, security pilot, Death Squad operative/commander/medic, RedWings pilots and medics) and faction badges that tag people as Nanotrasen, IOT or USSP combatants, medics or officers during faction-war events. On your own screen you also pick up new status alerts and effect icons that tell you when something is happening to your body rather than making you guess: internal bleeding, a combat technique that has slowed you down, a shield/blocking indicator, a temporary boost to how much damage you can take before going critical, and a generic countdown alert slot for timed effects.

- **Take** — It is the readability layer for everything else a fork adds: once you have extra jobs, ghost roles, faction events and custom status effects, players need icons and alerts that name them, and this is a ready-made pattern for all four at once.
- **Skip** — Almost every prototype here is a label for SS220 content you would not have (magistrate, RedWings, IOT/USSP factions, Death Squad, void blocker, the Frontier reagent, their pathology system), so grafting it lands you a pile of icons pointing at nothing plus C# status-effect components pulled in from unrelated fork systems.
- **Touches** — security, medical, command, antagonist, ghost-roles, all-players
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster), [gamemaster-and-ghost-roles](#gamemaster-and-ghost-roles), [medical-status-conditions](#medical-status-conditions), [martial-arts](#martial-arts), [melee-weapons-and-blocking](#melee-weapons-and-blocking)
- **Verified** — supported. Every specific icon/effect named in the gameplay paragraph traces directly to a fetched file: sechud.yml has exactly wanted/monitor/search/execute/demote/paroled/incarcerated/discharged; job.yml/centcom.yml have magistrate, SecurityPilot, and the three Deathsquad ranks; antag.yml has the NT/IOT/USSP faction icons (Combatant/Medic/Officer variants); movement.yml has the martial-art slowdown; blocking.yml has the shield icon; internal_blood_loss.yml has internal bleeding; mobThresholds.yml has the critical-threshold boost; categories.yml has the CountDown alert slot. On graft shape, the same evidence directly contradicts a plain drop-in reading even though no files sit outside SS220/ dirs: mobThresholds.yml names a 'Frontier' reagent dependency in a code comment, internal_blood_loss.yml wires in PathologyStatusEffectStackable, and every job/faction icon points at roles (magistrate, RedWings, Deathsquad, IOT/USSP combat factions) that only exist in other fork units. The claim's own skip_because and depends_on list match this evidence closely, so the entangled downgrade from the seed's 'drop-in' signal is correct, not an overclaim.

**stellar-station**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Instead of reading your condition from a handful of coarse icons, you get labelled sliding bars stacked around your character: a red Health bar that becomes a darker "Critical Health" bar once you go down and a grey "Dead" one when you die, a yellow Stamina bar for how much sprinting you have left, thin Hunger and Thirst bars on either side, a blue Respiration bar that drains as your air tank empties while internals are on, and a green Battery bar for anything that runs on a cell. Hovering a bar tells you plainly what it does ("If this gets to 0, you go critical", "The amount of gas left in the tank"). Alongside that, being incapacitated is spelled out in words rather than guessed at: a yellow "Stun Buildup" meter warns you that you are about to be knocked down and is already slowing you, and separate red "Stunned", "Slowed" and "Restrained" warnings tell you exactly why you cannot act, with "Restrained" also telling you that anyone dragging you will meet no resistance. Wielding a gun puts a crosshair on your cursor so you can see where you are actually aiming.

- **Take** — It turns the vaguest part of the game (how close am I to crit, how much air is left, why can't I move) into continuous labelled bars every player reads at a glance, and the bar itself is a data-driven prototype so a fork can add its own without touching C#.
- **Skip** — The resource bar files carry an SPDX header of `LicenseRef-Wallening`, a custom non-standard licence rather than MIT or AGPL, so grafting it into an AGPL fork is a licensing question before it is an engineering one; on top of that the Stunned / Stun Buildup / Restrained alerts are the readout for a fork stun-and-grab rework that does not exist upstream, so half the strings would have nothing to display.
- **Touches** — all-players, medical, security, silicons
- **Needs** — [close-quarters-combat-rework](#close-quarters-combat-rework)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**backmen**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Certain glasses and roles let you see floating health bars over living creatures, so you can read at a glance who is hurt and how badly instead of scanning them one by one. The HUD also gains a pile of new overhead markers: fellow vampires, blob members and flesh cultists recognise each other by an icon over their heads, medical HUDs flag anyone carrying an infection, extra job icons cover the fork's own roles (marines, sergeants, death squad), and the AI can paint a target that security sees. New status alerts sit in your alert tray too: a red "Face blocked!" warning you click to tear a creature off your face before you suffocate, cosmic heart/lung icons telling you that you can survive vacuum, and a power-level meter for shadowkin abilities.

- **Take** — Overhead health bars and faction icons change how fights and rescues are read at a glance, and the health-bar overlay itself is a compact, self-contained client feature.
- **Skip** — Most of the pack is icon and alert prototypes wired to Backmen's own antags, species, organs, jobs, mood and disease systems, so grafted alone it is one health-bar overlay plus a folder of icons that never appear.
- **Touches** — medical, security, command, antagonist, all-players
- **Needs** — [vampire](#vampire), [blob](#blob), [extra-playable-species](#extra-playable-species), [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster), [assigned-roleplay-moods](#assigned-roleplay-moods), [disease-and-virology](#disease-and-virology)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**forky**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 8 forks carry it*

Two visible things. First, everyone wearing a job-tracking HUD (security visors, medical glasses, command implants) now sees proper little job badges floating over the fork's extra staff instead of a blank or generic marker: Central Command Intern and Official, Corporate Liaison, Executive Officer, Hospitality Director, Internal Affairs Agent, Magistrate, Deputy, Fitness Instructor, Space Cowboy, Party Maker, Command Maid, Security Clown. Second, vending machines stop being a scrolling list and become an actual vending machine face: a lit grid of products each tagged with a letter-and-number code, sold-out slots flagged "SOLD OUT", and a numeric keypad you punch the code into, with CLR to wipe a mistake and ENT to buy. Each key chirps a different musical note, the display blinks a cursor, flashes "[ ENJOY ]" or "[ YUM! ]" on a successful vend, buzzes on a bad code, and times out and resets if you dither too long.

- **Take** — The keypad turns a mundane menu click into a small tactile ritual with sound and feedback, and it costs the round nothing: the same items come out.
- **Skip** — The job-icon half is dead weight without the fork jobs it labels (Magistrate, Corporate Liaison, Deputy and friends), and the keypad swaps out the vending machine interface via a client-side interface hook, so it needs upstream vending UI edits and will fight every upstream vending change afterwards.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, command, service, cargo
- **Needs** — [extra-job-roster](#extra-job-roster)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

### Visual feedback and particle effects

<a id="visual-feedback-effects"></a>

Screen and world feedback for what just happened: pain flashes, screenshake, interaction particles, sparks, a general particle emitter framework and cutscene playback.

**rmc14**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 10 forks carry it*

Fights and interactions get visible, readable punctuation: a punch throws up a little impact burst, a grab and a disarm each flash their own icon over the target, and social gestures like a fistbump, high-five, handshake or hug pop a small picture above the pair's heads for a few seconds so bystanders can see what happened without reading chat. Big hits and blasts knock your view around in a short series of jolts, explosions and burning leave scorch marks scattered across the floor afterwards, and creatures under a buff or in a special state glow with a coloured outline around their sprite (green, red or purple depending on what is affecting them). There are also warning markers that flash on the ground, such as a red exclamation over a pipe that is about to blow.

- **Take** — It makes melee, grabs, disarms and explosions legible at a glance, which is exactly the feedback SS14 combat is weakest at, and the coloured aura outline is a reusable way to show "this mob has a status on it" without a new HUD element.
- **Skip** — Almost none of it fires on its own: the effect entities are inert prototypes that RMC's own combat, xeno and emote code spawns, so grafting it means patching upstream melee, disarm and emote handlers to call the spawns, and the shared base effect prototype pulls in RMC's night-vision-visibility component, so you either bring that along or strip it and re-test every effect.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, antagonist, medical
- **Needs** — [screen-overlays-and-visors](#screen-overlays-and-visors)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**stellar-station**

*loop-change · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 10 forks carry it*

Hitting someone or something makes the world react: a little ghost image of whatever you're holding flings itself from your hand toward the target, tumbles and fades, and the thing you hit visibly flinches backward for a fraction of a second before snapping back. Powered gear, gunshots hitting metal, and things breaking throw off short-lived orange (or blue) sparks that light the area, make a crackling noise, bounce off walls and can set flammable stuff alight. Certain hits and effects also shove the camera around with a decaying shake. Separately, clicking on another player opens a small radial wheel around them instead of doing the default hand interaction, with options like grab/pull, examine, and a timed pat-down to inspect what they're wearing.

- **Take** — It makes every melee swing, tool use and gunshot feel like it landed, with almost none of the effect logic touching upstream game rules: the particle and recoil pieces just listen to interaction and damage-flash events that already fire.
- **Skip** — It arrives as three separate bundles glued together (Ephemeral Space sparks/screenshake, Stellar interaction particles, and a player-on-player radial menu) across two fork-only assembly projects; the sparks only appear once you sprinkle spark components onto upstream weapon and machine prototypes, and the radial menu intercepts the ordinary click-a-person interaction and pulls in the fork's social-interaction system, so taking the visuals without also taking that UI change means unpicking it.
- **Touches** — all-players, security, engineering, medical
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**serbia-strong**

*cosmetic · needs upstream edits · medium confidence · 10 forks carry it*

This is a grab-bag of on-screen feedback rather than one feature. Drugged or supermatter-exposed crew start seeing things that are not there: skeletons, graves, lightning, sparks, portals, gore, stray toolboxes and even a floating balloon, each pulled from a weighted table so no two trips look alike. Cult ritual drugs wash the screen in a pulsing tentacle-and-vignette overlay that fades in and out instead of a flat colour tint. Characters can sprout a thought bubble above their head, a ninja's whistle plants a loud exclamation marker over a target, and a player who dies gets a pop-up window asking them to confirm before their body is handled.

- **Take** — The reusable pieces (a fading/pulsing full-screen texture overlay, a weighted hallucination-spawn table, an animation helper, a thought bubble) are the kind of presentation plumbing every other fork feature ends up wanting, and they are cheap to reuse once present.
- **Skip** — It is not a coherent feature you can port in one go: the effect prototypes reference SS220's own drugs, cult, supermatter and gore entities, so grafting them without those systems leaves you with a framework and a pile of effects nothing ever triggers.
- **Touches** — all-players, medical, engineering, antagonist, science
- **Needs** — [reagent-and-reaction-expansion](#reagent-and-reaction-expansion), [supermatter-engine](#supermatter-engine)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.

**impstation**

*loop-change · entangled · medium confidence · 10 forks carry it*

This is a grab-bag of world-effect systems rather than one feature. The showpiece is a glowing crystal growth that creeps outward tile by tile after a supermatter incident: it lights the area a sickly yellow, cracks and groans as it spawns, replaces the floor plating underneath, and hurts anyone who walks onto it, so the crew has to route around a spreading no-go zone instead of just fleeing a boom. Alongside it: creatures and items that visibly change sprite the moment their holder flicks into combat mode (claws out, weapon raised), objects that puff chemical smoke on a timer or burst into a cloud when they vanish, a cleaning tool that scrubs blood and decals in a small radius while draining its own solution tank, and a set of spell effects (writhing dark tentacles that grab someone in place, plus joke event spells that rain soap or lollipops on the whole station).

- **Take** — The crystal cascade turns a supermatter failure from a single explosion into an ongoing, navigable hazard the whole crew has to work around, and the smaller pieces (combat-mode sprites, timed smoke emitters, radius decal cleaning) are generic building blocks you can hang on your own content.
- **Skip** — It is not one feature but five loosely related ones, and the best part of it does not stand alone: the crystal system compiles against an Einstein Engines supermatter component and a custom floor tile, and the spell effects assume a magic/action stack, so you inherit two large upstream systems to get one visual payoff.
- **Touches** — engineering, service, all-players, antagonist
- **Needs** — [supermatter-engine](#supermatter-engine), [wizard-magic](#wizard-magic)
- **Verification** — none; read this as a claim, not a finding.
